Causal Identification under Limited Additive Interventions

We now formalize the limited additive intervention regime. Recall from (
10
) and (
12
) that, under the additive intervention regime, the distributional heterogeneity of
(X^{e},Y^{e})
across environments is induced by the environment-specific interventions
\delta^{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
added to the covariates. The limited additive intervention regime arises when certain coordinates of
\delta^{e}
are identically zero across all environments, meaning that the corresponding covariates are never intervened. Formally, we define the set
S_{L}
for those coordinates that have been intervened upon as follows:

S_{L}:=\left\{j\in[p]:\exists~e\in\mathcal{E},\delta^{e}_{j}\not\equiv 0\right\},

(29)

and the limited additive intervention occurs whenever
S_{L}\neq[p]
, i.e., at least one covariate is never intervened upon among all environments.

In this regime, the earlier Condition
1b
no longer holds. Indeed, one can show that
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}])\mathbf{G}
, where
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
are full-rank matrices. Substituting this expression, Condition
1b
can be equivalently rephrased in terms of the intervention second moment matrices
\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}\delta^{e\intercal}]
: there exists a weight vector
w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
such that

\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succ 0,\quad\textrm{where}\quad\mathbf{\Delta}(w):=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}],\;\textrm{for any $w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}$}.

(30)

The details are deferred to Appendix Lemma
1
.
With limited intervention, at least one coordinate of
{\delta^{e}}
is always zero for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, making
\mathbf{\Delta}(w)
singular for all
w\in\Delta^{L}
, and thus (
30
) never holds.

To weaken Condition
1b
tailored for the limited interventions, instead of requiring the full matrix
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succ 0
, it suffices to ensure that the appropriate submatrix of
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})
be strictly positive definite. In particular, let
D\subseteq[p]
denote the set of covariates that are directly affected by the outcome, i.e., those for which there is a directed causal edge from
Y
to
X_{D}
. We propose the following condition:

Condition 4
.

D\subseteq S_{L}
and there exists a weight vector
w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
such that both of the following hold:

[\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})]_{S_{L},S_{L}}\succ 0,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ 0.

This condition imposes three requirements. First, it requires
D\subseteq S_{L}
, ensuring that every child of the outcome, that is, each covariate directly affected by the outcome, is intervened upon at least once across environments. Second, it requires the submatrix
[\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})]_{S_{L},S_{L}}
, restricted to the intervened coordinates, to be strictly positive definite, which is analogous to Condition
1b
. Finally, the requirement
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ 0
ensures that the aggregated covariates remain nondegenerate under the weighting
w^{0}
.
Together, these requirements guarantee that there is sufficient environmental heterogeneity among the causal children of the outcome.

This condition is strictly weaker than Condition
1b
.
In fact, when all covariates are intervened with
S_{L}=[p]
, Condition
4
reduces exactly to Condition
1b
.
To illustrate the difference, consider an experiment that begins with a reference environment with no interventions, and subsequently performs a series of single-covariate interventions at a time. As depicted in Figure
3
, there are
p=3
covariates, and only
X_{2}
is directly affected by the outcome
Y
, i.e.,
D=\{2\}
. Condition
4
requires intervention only on
X_{2}
, as it is the unique child of the outcome, whereas Condition
1b
requires interventions on all three covariates
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
.

Figure 3
:
Comparison of Conditions
4
and
1b
. Orange nodes indicate intervened covariates. Condition
4
requires interventions on the covariate directly affected by the outcome (
X_{2}
), whereas Condition
1b
requires interventions on all covariates
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
.

The following theorem shows that Condition
4
suffices for causal identification, i.e., the optimal solution of the constrained problem (
7
) coincides with
\beta^{*}
.

Theorem 6
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose there is no hidden confounding between the outcome and covariates, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=0
. If Condition
4
holds, then the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
achieves the smallest prediction risk among all risk-invariant prediction models. Consequently, the solution to the constrained problem (
7
) exactly recovers
\beta^{*}
.

Compared to the earlier Theorem
3
, which requires
\beta^{*}
to be the unique risk-invariant prediction model, this theorem shows that even if there are multiple risk-invariant prediction models, the objective of the constrained problem (
7
) ensures that
\beta^{*}
is singled out as the minimizer.

For clarity of exposition, this theorem focuses on the case without hidden confounding between the outcome and covariates, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=0
, as assumed in prior works
(
Hauser and Bühlmann, 2015
;
Peters et al., 2016
;
Ghassami et al., 2017
)
. We address the more general case, where there could exist hidden confounding in Appendix
C.2
. Furthermore, paralleling Theorem
4
,
we show in Appendix
C.1
that under Condition
4
,
the NegDRO objective (
9
) exhibits a benign nonconvex landscape for finite
\gamma\geq 0
under the limited intervention regime,
ensuring that gradient-based algorithms can still reliably approach
\beta^{*}
.

5.2
Comparison to Existing Methods

In the last subsection, we showed that the constrained problem (
7
) could recover
\beta^{*}
even under the challenging limited additive intervention regime. One may ask whether existing approaches can achieve the same. We focus on two representative methods, CausalDantzig
(
Rothenhäusler et al., 2019
)
and DRIG
(
Shen et al., 2025
)
, and show that they fail to identify
\beta^{*}
under limited interventions as their key assumptions no longer hold. Below, we provide a brief overview of these methods; a more detailed introduction is available in Appendix
C.3
.

CausalDantzig leverages the gradient invariance property inherent to the additive intervention regime, which is formalized in its Proposition 1. This property states that for any pair of environments
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
, the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
satisfies:

\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(Y^{e}-(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}X^{e})]=\mathbb{E}[X^{f}(Y^{f}-(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}X^{f})].

(31)

Assuming the non-singularity of
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}-X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]
, CausalDantzig identifies
\beta^{*}
through:

\beta^{*}=\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}-X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}Y^{e}-X^{f}Y^{f}].

However, this method fails under the limited additive intervention scenarios, since
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}-X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]
is singular when some covariates are never intervened upon across environments
e,f
.
In contrast, our approach remains effective in identifying
\beta^{*}
under such a limited additive intervention regime, once Condition
4
holds, as established in Theorem
6
.

As for DRIG, it relies on the existence of and access to a
reference environment
, denoted as
e=0
. This reference environment is assumed to be strictly dominated by all other environments
\mathcal{E}=\{1,2,...,|\mathcal{E}|\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[X^{(0)}X^{(0)\intercal}]\prec\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, which is a special case of our Condition
1a
. Given a regularization parameter
\gamma\geq 0
,
DRIG solves the following optimization problem:

b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma}=\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\mathbb{E}[Y^{0}-b^{\intercal}X^{0}]^{2}+\gamma\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\left(\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{0}-b^{\intercal}X^{0}]^{2}\right)\right\},

(32)

where
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
is a pre-specified weight vector. While the primary focus of DRIG is to ensure robust generalization to unseen populations for finite
\gamma
, the method can achieve causal discovery with
b_{\rm DRIG}^{\infty}=\beta^{*}
when
\gamma
is set as
\infty
, but only if such a strictly dominated reference environment exists and is accessible.

We emphasize that the reference environment plays a critical role in DRIG’s formulation. With access to such an environment
e=0
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]-\mathbb{E}[X^{0}(X^{0})^{\intercal}]\succ 0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Consequently, the optimization problem in (
32
) becomes convex, since its Hessian
\mathbb{E}[X^{0}(X^{0})^{\intercal}]+\gamma\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}(\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]-\mathbb{E}[X^{0}(X^{0})^{\intercal}])\succ 0
.
This convexity significantly simplifies the computational effort, as DRIG requires solving only a convex problem. However, it is challenging to identify a reference environment in practice. In fact, such a reference environment may not exist, limiting the practical application of the DRIG method. This is a key distinction from our proposed NegDRO in (
9
), which involves solving a nonconvex problem to an approximate global optimality without assuming the existence of, nor access to, a reference environment.

Next, we use the following Example
3
to compare the performance of NegDRO, CausalDantzig and DRIG under three variants of the additive intervention regimes:

(i)

Limited regime: only the outcome’s child covariates are intervened upon, while all non-child covariates remain untouched;

(ii)

Weak regime: non-child covariates are also intervened upon, but only with very small strength;

(iii)

Strong regime: all covariates are subject to sufficiently large interventions.

Figure 4
:
Illustration of Example
3
under the three additive intervention regimes. Solid orange nodes represent covariates with large-scale interventions, hollow orange nodes represent covariates with small-scale interventions, and white nodes indicate covariates with no interventions.

Example 3
.

Consider two environments
\mathcal{E}=\{1,2\}
. For each
e\in\mathcal{E}
, the variables are generated as follows:

X^{e}_{1}=\varepsilon^{e}_{1},\quad X^{e}_{2}=X^{e}_{1}+\varepsilon^{e}_{2},\quad Y^{e}=2X^{e}_{2}+\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\quad X^{e}_{3}=0.5X^{e}_{1}-Y^{e}+\varepsilon^{e}_{3},\quad X^{e}_{4}=\varepsilon^{e}_{4},

Among the four covariates,
X_{3}
is the only child of
Y
, i.e.
D=\{3\}
. The noise terms
\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}
and
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}=\varepsilon^{e}_{1:4}
are generated according to
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\varepsilon^{e}_{X})^{\intercal}=\eta+(0,\delta^{e})^{\intercal}
,
where
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0_{4},\mathbf{I}_{4})
denotes the systematic noise, and
\delta^{e}
denotes the environment-specific interventions.

In the environment
e=1
, no interventions are applied, i.e.,
\delta^{(1)}=0_{4}
.
In the environment
e=2
, the outcome’s child
X_{3}
is intervened with
\delta^{(2)}_{3}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2)
. For the remaining covariates, we examine three intervention regimes, which are illustrated in Figure
4
.

\textrm{(Limited)}~~\delta^{(2)}_{\{1,2,4\}}\equiv 0_{3};~~\textrm{(Weak)}~~\delta^{(2)}_{\{1,2,4\}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,0.01{\bf I}_{3});~~\textrm{(Strong)}~~\delta^{(2)}_{\{1,2,4\}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,0.25{\bf I}_{3}).

Figure
5
summarizes the numerical results across three additive intervention regimes. For NegDRO and DRIG, we evaluate the distance of the fitted estimators to the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
against the regularization parameter
\gamma
varying within the range
[0,60]
. Since CausalDantzig does not include a regularization parameter, its performance remains unchanged across different
\gamma
values.

The figure shows that under the limited and weak regimes, DRIG estimators deviate from
\beta^{*}
as
\gamma
increases. Similarly, CausalDantzig fails to approximate
\beta^{*}
in these two regimes. Only in the strong intervention setting do both CausalDantzig and DRIG (with a relatively large
\gamma
) achieve an accurate approximation of
\beta^{*}
. In contrast, the proposed NegDRO consistently delivers estimators close to
\beta^{*}
across all three regimes.

Figure 5
:
Comparison of NegDRO with CausalDantzig and DRIG in terms of
\ell_{2}
distance of estimators computed by these methods to the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
. Sample size
n_{e}=10,000
for
e\in\mathcal{E}
. For NegDRO and DRIG, we vary the regularization parameter
\gamma
in the range of
[0,60]
, while CausalDantzig does not require it. The results are averaged over 200 simulations.

6
Numerical Results

In this section, we evaluate the performance of the proposed NegDRO through simulated experiments. We consider a synthetic example whose causal structure is illustrated in Figure
6
.

Figure 6
:
Causal structure of (
33
): the highlighted covariates
(X_{1},X_{3})
are direct causes of
Y
.

For each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}=\{1,2,3,4\}
, the covariates
X^{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and outcome
Y^{e}\in\mathbb{R}
are generated from the following structural equations:

\begin{gathered}X^{e}_{1}=\varepsilon^{e}_{1},~~X^{e}_{2}=X^{e}_{1}+\varepsilon^{e}_{2},~~X^{e}_{3}=X^{e}_{2}+\varepsilon^{e}_{3},\\
Y^{e}=\frac{1}{2}X^{e}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}X^{e}_{3}+\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\\
X^{e}_{4}=Y^{e}+\varepsilon^{e}_{4},~~X^{e}_{5}=-Y^{e}+\varepsilon^{e}_{5},~~X^{e}_{6:p}=\varepsilon^{e}_{6:p}.\end{gathered}

(33)

The causal set is
S^{*}=\{1,3\}
with the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}=(\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2},0_{p-3})^{\intercal}
.

According to the relationships to
Y
, the covariates can be partitioned into three groups: (i) true causes
(X_{1},X_{3})
, which directly determine
Y
; (ii) correlated but non-causal covariates
(X_{2},X_{4},X_{5})
; and (iii) irrelevant covariates
X_{6:p}
, where we vary
p
to adjust the dimension of the covariates without changing the underlying causal structure.

The noise terms
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\varepsilon^{e}_{1:p})^{\intercal}
follow a structural additive form
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\varepsilon^{e}_{1:p})^{\intercal}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\eta+(0,{\delta^{e}})^{\intercal}
, where
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0_{p+1},\mathbf{I}_{p+1})
is the systematic noise component, and
\delta^{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are the environment-specific interventions. To introduce environmental heterogeneity, we vary the intervention strengths across environments:

\delta^{1}_{1:5}=0_{5};~~\delta^{2}_{1:5}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,9);~~\delta^{3}_{1:5}=(1,1.5,2,2.5,3)^{\intercal};~~\delta^{4}_{1:5}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\textrm{Unif}(-0.5,0.5),

while the irrelevant coordinates follow
\delta^{e}_{6:p}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\tfrac{e^{2}}{4}\cdot{\bf I}_{p-5})
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

For each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we generate i.i.d. samples
\{x^{e}_{i},y^{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{e}}
with equal sample sizes
n_{e}=n
.
Note that in this setup, we focus on the scenario without hidden confounding between the covariates and the outcome, whereas additional experiments that incorporate hidden confounders are provided in Appendix
F.4
.
The following numerical study consists of two parts: (i) we evaluate the accuracy of the proposed NegDRO under varying model parameters and sample sizes, and (ii) we examine both the estimation accuracy and runtime of NegDRO relative to existing approaches under varying dimensions.

Dependence on
\gamma,n,T
.

Figure
7
illustrates how the estimation error
\|\hat{b}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
varies with the regularization parameter
\gamma
, the sample size
n
, and the number of iterations
T
, where
\hat{b}
denotes the output of Algorithm
1
. The covariate dimension varies across
p\in\{5,10,40\}
.
In the leftmost panel, we fix the sample size at
n=20{,}000
and the number of iterations at
T=1000
, while varying
\gamma
within
[0,17]
.
In the middle panel,
\gamma=20
and
T=1000
are fixed, and the sample size
n
ranges from
100
to
20{,}000
.
In the rightmost panel, we fix
n=20{,}000
and
\gamma=20
, while varying
T
from
0
to
1000
.
The results collectively show that the proposed Algorithm
1
converges toward the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
as
\gamma
,
n
, and
T
increase, consistent with the theoretical guarantees established in Theorem
5
.

Figure 7
:
Dependence of
\|\hat{b}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
on the regularization parameter
\gamma
, the sample size
n
, and the number of iterations, where
\hat{b}
is the output of Algorithm
1
.
These results are averaged over 200 simulations.

Scalability with Dimension
p
.

We compare our NegDRO with other causal invariance learning methods in terms of both estimation accuracy (measured as the
\ell_{2}
distance to the causal effect
\beta^{*}
) and computational cost (measured in seconds), where the dimension
p
is varied in the range of
[5,100]
. The methods included in the comparison are
ICP

(
Peters et al., 2016
)
,
Anchor

(
Rothenhäusler et al., 2021
)
, and
EILLS

(
Fan et al., 2024
)
. We also include
ERM
method for comparative purposes, which pools all observed data across environments and fits a least-squares model.
We set the regularization parameter
\gamma=20
for our NegDRO. Following the recommendation in their original work,
EILLS
also uses a hyperparameter
\gamma=20
. For other causal invariance learning approaches, we choose the best hyperparameters (if applicable), i.e., we enumerate a wide range of hyperparameter values and select the best one that minimizes
\ell_{2}
distance to
\beta^{*}
. Detailed implementations for all methods are in Appendix
F.4
.

Figure
8
summarizes the results regarding estimation error versus dimension and runtime versus dimension.
To ensure timely execution, we impose a 30-minute time limit for each method; if a method does not finish within this limit, it terminates automatically.
In the left panel of Figure
8
, among the evaluated methods, only our NegDRO closely approximates
\beta^{*}
, irrespective of the covariate dimension
p
. Notably,
ELLIS
performs exceptionally well in lower-dimensional settings when
p\leq 20
, yielding the smallest
\ell_{2}
distance compared to all other methods. However, its computational time reaches the 30-minute time limit when
p\geq 25
, since its complexity scales exponentially with dimension due to enumeration. We also observe
ELLIS
deteriorates significantly in the presence of hidden confounders; see Appendix
F.4
.
The
ICP
method, which prioritizes selecting direct causes through hypothesis testing, demonstrates conservative behavior in this setting, resulting in poor accuracy for estimating
\beta^{*}
. Additionally,
Anchor
provides results nearly identical to those of
ERM
, and both have significant divergence from
\beta^{*}
.

The right panel of Figure
8
depicts the average runtime for each method, with a black dashed horizontal line marking the 30-minute time limit. Methods exceeding this limit are terminated. It is evident that
ICP
and
EILLS
, which rely on exhaustive search enumeration, quickly exceed the time limit as the covariate dimension
p
increases to
15
and
25
, respectively. In contrast, all other methods, including our proposed NegDRO, demonstrate computational costs that scale polynomially with the dimension
p
.

Figure 8
:
Comparison of different causal invariance learning methods. The dimension
p
varies within the range of
[5,100]
, while the sample size is fixed at
n=20,000
. Results are averaged over 200 simulations.

7
Conclusion

We propose the NegDRO formulation for causal invariance learning, which frames it as a nonconvex continuous minimax problem. Building on this formulation, our work addresses two fundamental questions: (i) under what conditions on environmental heterogeneity can we achieve causal identification, and (ii) how can
\beta^{*}
be efficiently achieved despite the inherent nonconvexity of NegDRO. Unlike existing causal invariance learning approaches that rely on enumeration and thus face exponential computational costs in the covariate dimension
p
, our method scales mildly to higher dimensions.

This work lies at the intersection of statistics and optimization. From the optimization perspective, the proposed NegDRO reformulates the statistical causal identification problem, transforming the combinatorial search for invariant subsets into a smooth minimax problem amenable to gradient-based methods. From a statistical perspective, the identification conditions reveal the geometric structure of the nonconvex optimization landscape, ensuring that the stationary points are statistically meaningful. Together, these insights demonstrate how optimization redefines and solves statistical causal inference, while statistical principles, in turn, endow optimization with an interpretable geometric structure.

Appendix to “Causal Invariance Learning via Efficient Nonconvex Optimization”.

The appendix is structured as follows:

•

Appendix
A
provides the proofs of the main theoretical results presented in the main article.

•

Appendix
B
analyzes the unpenalized NegDRO formulation, which is nonconvex and nonsmooth, providing results parallel to those in Section
4
.

•

Appendix
C
presents additional discussions and theoretical insights on the role of the optimization objective, complementing Section
5
of the main text.

•

Appendix
D
includes the full proofs of the results introduced in Appendices
A
,
B
, and
C
.

•

Appendix
E
contains the proofs of technical lemmas.

•

Appendix
F
offers additional illustrations of the structural equation models (SEMs) and further discussions on the simulation studies.

We provide a detailed Table of Contents for the Appendix below to facilitate navigation of the supplementary materials.

Appendix A
Proofs of Main Results

This section contains the proofs of the theoretical results presented in the main article. It is outlined as follows:

•

A.1
presents the expression of environmental risks within the additive intervention regime. It prepares for the theoretical analysis in later sections.

•

A.2
-
A.5
provide proofs of the theoretical results in Section
3
.

•

A.6
-
A.7
contain proofs of results within Sections
4.1
and
4.2
.

•

A.8
presents the proofs of results in Section
5
.

A.1
Preparation

Under the additive intervention regime, we shall present the expressions of the population risk
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
and empirical risk
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
, for a given predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

We start with the population risk
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
. The proof of the following lemma is provided in Appendix
E.1
.

Lemma 1
.

Under the additive intervention, it holds that

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]=-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h,\quad\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}.

Then, for a given predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the population risk
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
is expressed as follows:

\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2(\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h)^{\intercal}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}\delta^{e\intercal}])\mathbf{G}\right](b-\beta^{*}),

where

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}=\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1},\quad h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}],\quad\mathbf{H}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\eta\eta^{\intercal}]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}.\end{gathered}

Here, the matrix
\mathbf{G}
is a non-singular matrix, and
\mathbf{H}\succeq 0.

Through this lemma, we observe that under the additive intervention regime, the first-order term of the population risk is invariant across environments, i.e.,
(\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h)^{\intercal}(b-\beta^{*})
is independent of
e
. Indeed, the only difference in environmental risks lies in the second-order term, which purely depends on the additive component
\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}\delta^{e\intercal}]
.

Now we turn to studying the empirical environmental risk. For each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we have observations
\{x^{e}_{i},y^{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{e}}
i.i.d drawn from the distribution of
(X^{e},Y^{e}).

The following lemma presents the expression of
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[y^{e}_{i}-b^{\intercal}x^{e}_{i}]^{2}
, for a given predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. The proof of the following lemma is provided in Appendix
E.2
.

Lemma 2
.

Under the additive intervention, the empirical risk
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
, for a given predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, is expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\hat{r}^{e})+2[\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}(h-\hat{q}^{e})]^{\intercal}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]+\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}),

where
h,\mathbf{G}^{\intercal},\mathbf{H}
are defined in Lemma
1
, and
\hat{r}^{e},\hat{q}^{e},\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}
are terms capturing the finite-sample error, with expressions given in the following (
101
).

Notice that the terms
\hat{r}^{e},\hat{q}^{e},\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}
arise from the finite sample error, and thus shall be controlled with high probability, as demonstrated in the following Lemma. The proof is provided in Appendix
E.3
.

Lemma 3
.

Under the additive interventions, suppose Condition
2
holds. Then for any
s\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
, we have

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\max\{\|\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\|_{2},\|\hat{q}^{e}\|_{2},|\hat{r}^{e}|\}\lesssim{\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{{n}}}}+\frac{p+s}{{n}},

where
{n}=\min_{e\in\mathcal{E}}n_{e}
is the smallest sample size across all environments.

A.2
Proof of Theorem
1

We focus on the risk-invariant predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{f}-b^{\intercal}X^{f}]^{2}
for any
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
. Then it follows from Lemma
1
that, the risk-invariant predictor
b
satisfies that: for any
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
,

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}](b-\beta^{*}).

Since the above equation holds for any two environments
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
, the following equation must hold for any two collections of environments
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, for any weights
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|_{1}}
and
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}^{|\mathcal{E}_{2}|}
:

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w_{f}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*}).

(34)

By Condition
1a
, there exist two nonempty and disjoint collections of environments
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, and some weights
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|_{1}}
and
w^{\prime}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|_{2}}
such that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w_{f}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}].

If
b\neq\beta^{*}
, then it holds that

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})>(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w_{f}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*}).

which contradicts (
34
). Therefore, if
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a risk-invariant predictor satisfying
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{f}-b^{\intercal}X^{f}]^{2}
for all
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
, then it must hold that
b=\beta^{*}
. Therefore,

\mathcal{B}_{\rm inv}=\left\{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{f}-b^{\intercal}X^{f}]^{2},\;\forall e,f\in\mathcal{E}\right\}=\{\beta^{*}\}.

A.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We prove that if at most one coordinate of the covariate noise vector is intervened in each environment and the unique risk-invariant predictor is
\beta^{*}
, then Condition
1a
necessarily holds.

Step-1: Representation of the intervention structure.

By assumption, in each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
, at most one coordinate of noise vector
\delta^{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is intervened, while all other remain unchanged:

\left|\{j\in[p]:\delta^{e}_{j}\equiv 0\}\right|\geq p-1.

Consequently, the second-moment matrix of the intervention has the form:

\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]={\rm diag}(0,...,0,c^{e},0,...,0),

(35)

where the scalar
c^{e}\geq 0
denotes the intervention strength at coordinate
s^{e}\in[p]
, the index of the intervened variable in environment
e
.
Let

S_{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcup_{e\in\mathcal{E}}s^{e},\quad{C}_{\mathcal{E}}=\left\{c^{e}\right\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}}.

(36)

collect all intervened coordinates and their corresponding strengths.

Step-2: Characterizing risk invariance.

For any risk-invariant predictor
b\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm inv}
, Lemma
1
implies that for every pair of environments
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
:

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}\delta^{e\intercal}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\delta^{f}\delta^{f\intercal}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}).

Provided
\mathcal{B}_{\rm inv}=\{\beta^{*}\}
, i.e.
\beta^{*}
is the unique risk-invariant predictor, we know that for any
b\neq\beta^{*}
, the above equation does not hold. Since
\mathbf{G}
is a full rank-matrix as defined in Lemma
1
, this equivalently means that:

\{0_{p}\}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:~~u^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]u=u^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]u,~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$}\right\}.

In words, the zero vector is the only
u
whose quadratic forms are equal across all environments.

Using (
35
), this condition reduces to

\{0_{p}\}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:~~c^{e}[u_{s^{e}}]^{2}=c^{f}[u_{s^{f}}]^{2},\quad\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$}\right\}.

(37)

In the remaining proofs, we will leverage (
37
) to construct two collections of environments
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
required by Condition
1a
.

Step-3: Show that all coordinates must be intervened.

Suppose that some coordinates have never been intervened, i.e.
S_{\mathcal{E}}\subsetneq[p]
. Define
u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
by

\begin{cases}u_{j}=0&\textrm{if $j\in S_{\mathcal{E}}$}\\
u_{j}=1&\textrm{if $j\in S_{\mathcal{E}}^{\complement}$}\end{cases}.

Then
u\neq 0_{p}
and for all
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
,

c^{e}[u_{s^{e}}]^{2}=c^{f}[u_{s^{f}}]^{2}=0,

contradicting (
37
). Hence, every coordinate of the covariates must be intervened in at least one environment, such that

S_{\mathcal{E}}=[p].

(38)

We now distinguish two situations:

(Case-1)

at least one environment is unintervened.

(Case-2)

every environment is intervened (but possibly on different coordinates or scales).

Step-4: Construction for Case 1.

Without loss of generality, let environment
1
be unintervened such that
{\delta^{1}}\equiv 0_{p}
. Since
S_{\mathcal{E}}=[p]
by (
38
), the remaining environments
\{2,...,|\mathcal{E}|\}
collectively perturb all coordinates

\bigcup_{e=2}^{|\mathcal{E}|}s^{e}=[p].

Therefore,

\sum_{e\in\{2,3,...,|\mathcal{E}|\}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|-1}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\succ\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{1}}{\delta^{1\intercal}}]=0.

Because
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}])\mathbf{G}
, it follows that

\sum_{e\in\{2,3,...,|\mathcal{E}|\}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|-1}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\mathbb{E}[X^{1}X^{1\intercal}].

Hence, by letting

\mathcal{E}_{1}=\{2,3,...,|\mathcal{E}|\},\quad\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{1\},

Condition
1a
is satisfied.

Step-5: Construction for Case 2.

Step-5(a): Establishing a key claim.

We shall show that even if every environment intervenes some coordinate, there must exist two environments perturbing the same coordinate with different magnitudes. Specifically, we claim that if (
37
) holds, then there exists two environments
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
and a coordinate
s\in[p]
such that

\exists e,f\in\mathcal{E},\;{\rm s.t.}\;s^{e}=s^{f}=s~~\textrm{for some $s\in[p]$},~~\textrm{and}~~c^{e}\neq c^{f}.

(39)

We prove this by contradiction by considering the following two scenarios.

•

Scenario 1: suppose
s^{e}\neq s^{f}
for all pairs
e\neq f
. Then define
u_{s^{e}}=1/\sqrt{c^{e}}
for each
e\in\mathcal{E}
. This nonzero
u
satisfies
c^{e}[u_{s^{e}}]^{2}=1
for all
e
, contradicting (
37
). Thus this scenario is impossible.

•

Scenario 2: suppose some coordinates are shared among environments but with identical strength, i.e. whenever
s^{e}=s^{f}
, we also have
c^{e}=c^{f}
. Such a pair of environments has no heterogeneity and can be aggregated, reducing the problem to Scenario 1, which we have already ruled out.

Combining these two scenarios, we conclude that (
39
) must hold.

Step-5(b): Constructing two collections
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

By (
39
), there exist
e,f\in\mathcal{E}
intervening the same coordinate but with different scales.
Without loss of generality, we consider
s^{e}=s^{f}=1
with
0<c^{e}<c^{f}
. Thus

\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]={\rm diag}(c^{e},0_{p-1}),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]={\rm diag}(c^{f},0_{p-1}).

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{e\}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}\setminus\{e\}
.
Construct the weight vector
w=(w_{g})_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|-1}
as

w_{f}=\frac{tc^{e}}{c^{f}},\quad w_{g}=\left(\frac{c^{f}-tc^{e}}{(|\mathcal{E}|-2)c^{f}}\right)_{|\mathcal{E}|-2}\;\;\textrm{for }g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\setminus\{f\}.

where
t\in(1,\frac{c^{f}}{c^{e}}).
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{g}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{g}}{\delta^{g\intercal}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{tc^{e}}{c^{f}}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]+\frac{c^{f}-tc^{e}}{(|\mathcal{E}|-2)c^{f}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\setminus\{f\}}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{g}}{\delta^{g\intercal}}]

(40)

\displaystyle=\frac{tc^{e}}{c^{f}}{\rm diag}(c^{f},0_{p-1})+\frac{c^{f}-tc^{e}}{(|\mathcal{E}|-2)c^{f}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\setminus\{f\}}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{g}}{\delta^{g\intercal}}]

\displaystyle\succ{\rm diag}(c^{e},0_{p-1})=\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}].

where the inequality holds because
t>1
increases the first diagonal and, by (
38
), the remaining environments perturb all other coordinates.

Consequently, the dominance relation in Condition
1a
holds with

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\{e\}}w_{g}\mathbb{E}[X^{g}X^{g\intercal}]\succ\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}].

Step-6: Conclusion.

Both Case 1 and Case 2 lead to the existence of two disjoint, nonempty environment collections
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
such that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w_{f}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}],

which verifies Condition
1a
.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
3

It follows from Lemma
1
that the constrained optimization problem in (
7
) is equivalently expressed as:

\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}-b^{\intercal}X^{1}]^{2},\quad\textrm{s.t.}\;\;(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*})=0,\;\forall w\in\Delta^{L}.

If Condition
1b
holds, then the
\beta^{*}
is the only feasible solution satisfying
(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*})=0
for all
w\in\Delta^{L}
. Therefore, it holds that the constrained optimization problem recovers
\beta^{*}
exactly.

A.5
Proof of Proposition
1

First, we show that Condition
1b
implies Condition
1a
. If Condition
1b
holds, then it holds that

\bm{A}(w^{0})=\sum_{l\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\sum_{l\in\mathcal{E}}\left[(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})_{+}-(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})_{-}\right]\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ 0,

(41)

where we use
x_{+}=\max(x,0)
and
x_{-}=\max(-x,0)
to denote the positive part and negative part for a real number
x
.
Since
w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, it must hold that
\sum_{l\in\mathcal{E}}(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})=0
. Thus,

\sum_{l\in\mathcal{E}}(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})_{+}=\sum_{l\in\mathcal{E}}(w_{l}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})_{-}=C,

(42)

for some constant
C>0.

Subsequently, we define two collections of environments
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\complement}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
:

\mathcal{G}=\{e\in\mathcal{E}:w_{e}^{0}>\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{G}^{\complement}=\{e\in\mathcal{E}:w_{e}^{0}\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\}.

It follows from (
41
) and (
42
) that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{w_{e}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{G}^{\complement}}\frac{1/|\mathcal{E}|-w_{f}^{0}}{C}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}].

We let
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{G}^{\complement}
,
then the previous equation shows that:

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w^{\prime}_{f}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}],

where
w_{e}=\frac{w_{e}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}
, and
w^{\prime}_{f}=\frac{1/|\mathcal{E}|-w_{f}^{0}}{C}.
And it holds that
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}_{1}|}
and
{w^{\prime}}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}_{2}|}.
Therefore, we show Condition
1a
holds.

Next, we prove that Condition
1a
implies Condition
1b
.
Given two weight vectors
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}_{1}|}
and
w^{\prime}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}_{2}|}
satisfying Condition
1a
, we define the constant
C=\min\left\{\frac{1-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}},\frac{1/|\mathcal{E}|}{\max_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}w^{\prime}_{f}}\right\}
. Clearly,
C>0
. Next, we shall construct a weight
w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
satisfying Condition
1b
.

•

For environment
e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we let
w^{0}_{e}=Cw_{e}+1/|\mathcal{E}|
;

•

For environment
f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we let
w^{0}_{f}=1/|\mathcal{E}|-C{w^{\prime}_{f}};

•

For environment
g\notin\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we let
w^{0}_{g}=1/|\mathcal{E}|
.

It is evident that any
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
w_{e}^{0}\in[0,1]
and
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}=1
, which implies that
w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
.
By Condition
1a
, we have

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}w_{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}{w^{\prime}_{f}}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}].

We plug in the constructed
w^{0}
, and it follows that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}\left(\frac{w_{e}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}\left(\frac{1/|\mathcal{E}|-w_{f}^{0}}{C}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}].

Multiplying the constant
C
on both sides, and adding the components regarding
g\notin\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we obtain that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}}\left(\frac{w_{e}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]+\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}\left(\frac{w_{f}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}]+\sum_{g\notin\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}}\left(\frac{w_{g}^{0}-1/|\mathcal{E}|}{C}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{g}X^{(g)\intercal}]\succ 0,

which implies that

\bm{A}(w^{0})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}(w_{e}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|})\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ 0.

Thus, Condition
1b
is met.

A.6
Proof of Theorem
4

Although Section
4.3
provides the main idea of the proof, this section presents the details for the proof of Theorem
4
.
We first provide the expressions of the objective
\Phi_{\mu}(b)
in (
19
).
It follows from Lemma
1
that

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

(43)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left(\sigma_{Y}^{2}-2(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left(\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*}),

where the vector
h\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the full-rank matrix
\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
are specified in Lemma
1
, and the last equality holds due to
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}.

Provided the maximizer
\bar{w}
defined in (
20
), we establish that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}

(44)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})0(b-\beta^{*})-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}.

Step-1:
Express
\Phi_{\mu}(b)
through its gradient
\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)
.

By Danskin’ theorem,
\Phi_{\mu}(b)
is differentiable with its gradient
\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)
given by:

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)=\frac{2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h+2\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+2\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*}).

Left multiplying the vector
\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})
, we establish that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*}).

Combining the above equality with (
44
), we establish that

\Phi_{\mu}(b)=\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}.

(45)

Step-2:
Establish the lower bound of
\Phi_{\mu}(b)
via the weight
w^{0}
specified in Condition
1b
.

Since
\bar{w}
is the maximizer weight as defined in (
20
), we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w^{0})(b-\beta^{*})-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succeq 0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, and
\bm{A}(w^{0})\succeq\lambda\mathbf{I}
as specified in Condition
1b
, we further establish that

\Phi_{\mu}(b)\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

(46)

Step-3:
Combine the results in the steps 1 and 2. It follows from (
45
) and (
46
) that

\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

By reorganizing and applying Chebyshev’s inequality, we shall establish that

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}\|h\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}\right)\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
w^{0},\bar{w}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, it follows that

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\left\|\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}\right)\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+\mu.

It further implies that

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\left\|\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}\right)+C\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}.

The proof is complete.

A.7
Proof of Theorem
5

The proof proceeds as follows.

•

We first establish the landscape of the empirical NegDRO, where we upper bound
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
through the gradient norm
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\|_{2}
for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

•

Second, we study the optimization convergence of Algorithm
1
. Specifically, for the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
, we shall control the gradient norm
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}
.

•

Lastly, we combine these two results to complete the proof.

First, we introduce the following theorem to construct a finite-sample bound for
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
, for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. The proof is provided in Section
D.1
.

Theorem 7

(Landscape of Empirical NegDRO)
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Conditions
1b
,
2
hold and
n\geq(1\vee c_{1}/\lambda^{2})p
. Then, for any
0\leq s\leq(1\wedge c_{2}\lambda^{2})n-p
, with a probability of at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
, the following inequality holds uniformly for all predictors
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

\left\|b-\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\|_{2}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{{n}}\right)^{1/4},

(47)

where
\lambda>0
denotes the degree of environmental heterogeneity defined in Condition
1b
, and
c_{1},c_{2}
are some absolute constants.

Compared to the population-level result established in Theorem
4
, the roles of the parameters
\gamma\geq 0
and
\mu>0
remain consistent. The only difference lies in the introduction of an additional finite-sample error term of order
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/4})
within the upper bound in (
47
).

Second, we show that Algorithm
1
converges to a stationary point of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\cdot)
with an increasing number of iterations. The proof of the following proposition is provided in Section
D.2
.

Proposition 3
.

Suppose Condition
3
holds. Then
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
is
L_{\widehat{\Phi}}
-smooth in
b
, with

L_{\widehat{\Phi}}=2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu+\frac{(2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu)^{2}}{2\mu}.

By setting the step size in Algorithm
1
as
\alpha=1/L_{\widehat{\Phi}}\asymp(\mu+(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|})/\mu)^{-1}
, the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
of Algorithm
1
satisfies:

\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\right\|_{2}^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}|\mathcal{E}|}{\mu}+\mu\right)}.

The result aligns with similar findings on stationary convergence rates of gradient descent methods in the literature
[
Bottou et al., 2018
]
. Notably, this stationary convergence rate is optimal for first-order methods, as established by
Carmon et al. [2020]
,
Nesterov et al. [2018]
.

Lastly, we combine Theorem
7
and Proposition
3
to conclude that:

\displaystyle\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{{n}}\right)^{1/4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}|\mathcal{E}|}{\mu}+\mu\right)}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{{n}}\right)^{1/4}.

A.8
Proof of Theorem
6

Recall the constrained problem (
7
) as follows:

\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}-b^{\intercal}X^{1}]^{2}\quad{\rm s.t}\quad\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{f}-b^{\intercal}X^{f}]^{2}\;\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$}.

We apply the expression of the risk
\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
in Lemma
1
and establish the equivalent formulation of the constrained problem:

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})

(48)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\quad(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}](b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$},

where the explicit expressions of
h
and
\mathbf{G}
are given in Lemma
1
.

The remaining proof proceeds as follows:

Step-1
:

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Condition
4
holds. We show that for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
subject to the constraint within (
48
) satisfies that
h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0
.

Step-2
:

Based on Step-1, we simplify the objective function within (
48
), and prove that solving (
48
) exactly identifies
\beta^{*}
.

We now detail the remaining proof.

Step-1:

We observe that the following equation holds for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
subject to the constraint within (
48
):

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})=0,\quad\forall w\in\Delta^{L}.

We then apply Lemma
1
, which shows that
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}
for
\mathbf{G},\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
being full-rank matrices, to obtain that

[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0,\quad\forall w\in\Delta^{L}.

Recall that we define
S_{L}
as the set with interventions such that
S_{L}=\{j\in[p]:\;\exists e\in\mathcal{E},\;\delta^{e}_{j}\not\equiv 0\}
, indicating that
\delta^{e}_{j}\equiv 0
for all
j\notin S_{L}
and
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Therefore, the preceding equality further implies that

[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]^{\intercal}_{S_{L}}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}_{S_{L}}\delta^{e\intercal}_{S_{L}}]\right][\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}=0,\quad\forall w\in\Delta^{L}.

As Condition
4
requires the existence of a weight vector
w_{0}\in\Delta^{L}
satisfying that

[\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})]_{S_{L},S_{L}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}^{0}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}_{S_{L}}\delta^{e\intercal}_{S_{L}}]\succ 0,

we obtain that

[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}=0.

Since
S_{L}
contains all direct children of the outcome with
D\subseteq S_{L}
, it further implies that

[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{D}=0.

Recall the vector
h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]
as defined in Lemma
1
. When
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=0
, it holds

h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}.

Moreover,
\mathbf{B}_{YX}
denotes the coefficients from
Y
to covariates with support
{\rm supp}(\mathbf{B}_{YX})=D
. Therefore, we have
[\mathbf{B}_{YX}]_{D^{\complement}}=0
, and thus

\displaystyle h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

(49)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{Y}^{2}[\mathbf{B}_{YX}]_{D}^{\intercal}[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{D}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}[\mathbf{B}_{YX}]_{D^{\complement}}^{\intercal}[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{D^{\complement}}

\displaystyle=0.

Step 2:
Based on (
49
), we present the equivalent formulation of the problem (
48
) by removing the first-order term with respect to
b
in its objective function:

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\quad(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}](b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$},

Due to the risk-invariance constraint and
w^{0}\in\Delta^{L}
, equivalently, we have

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right)(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\quad(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}](b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{f}X^{f\intercal}](b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$},

We observe that
b=\beta^{*}
is a feasible point to the above optimization problem with the objective value
0
. And for any other feasible points
b\neq\beta^{*}
, the corresponding objective value satisfies that

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right)(b-\beta^{*})>0,

as we consider that there exists an environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
with
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succ 0
. Consequently, we obtain that the optimal solution is exactly
\beta^{*}
.

Appendix B
Unpenalized Nonsmooth NegDRO

This section introduces the unpenalized NegDRO problem. Compared to the penalized version discussed in the main text, the unpenalized NegDRO is not only nonconvex but also nonsmooth. Specifically, recall the population NegDRO, defined in (
17
), as follows:

\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Phi(b),\quad\textrm{where}\quad\Phi(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}.

The objective function of
\Phi(b)
may admit multiple maximizers; thus,
\Phi(b)
is not necessarily differentiable.
Correspondingly, the empirical NegDRO problem is defined as follows:

\min_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\widehat{\Phi}(b),\quad\textrm{where}\quad\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}.

(50)

Still,
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
may not be differentiable.
While most theoretical properties for the unpenalized NegDRO remain consistent with those in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, they require a more general definition of stationary points due to the nonsmooth nature of
\Phi(b)
and
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
.
To facilitate discussions, we introduce the concepts of subdifferential and generalized stationary points.

Definition 3

(Subgradient and Subdifferential)
.

Given a function
h:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, a vector
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is called a subgradient of
h
at a point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
if it satisfies:

h(b^{\prime})\geq h(b)+\zeta^{\intercal}(b^{\prime}-b)+o(\|b^{\prime}-b\|_{2}),~~\textrm{for all $b^{\prime}$ such that $b^{\prime}\rightarrow b$}.

The subdifferential of
h
at
b
, denoted as
\partial h(b)
, is the set of all subgradients at
b
:

\partial h(b)=\left\{\left.\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\right|h(b^{\prime})\geq h(b)+\zeta^{\intercal}(b^{\prime}-b)+o(\|b^{\prime}-b\|_{2}),~~\textrm{for all $b^{\prime}$ such that $b^{\prime}\rightarrow b$}\right\}.

The distance of subdifferential
\partial h(b)
to the origin is given by

{\rm Dist}(0,\partial h(b))=\inf\{\|\zeta\|_{2},~~\zeta\in\partial h(b)\}.

The subdifferential generalizes the notation of gradient.
If the function
h(\cdot)
is differentiable at the point
b
, the subdifferential reduces to its gradient with
\partial h(b)=\{\nabla h(b)\}
,
and its distance to the original becomes the gradient norm such that
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial h(b))=\|\nabla h(b)\|_{2}
. Building upon the definition of subdifferential, we define generalized stationary points as follows.

Definition 4

(Generalized Stationary Point)
.

A point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a (generalized) stationary point of the function
h:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
if and only if
0\in\partial h(b)
, that is,
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial h(b))=0
.

We emphasize that this definition extends the classic stationary point for differentiable functions, as defined in Definition
2
, where a point
b
is a stationary point of the differentiable function
h(\cdot)
if and only if
\|\nabla h(b)\|_{2}=0
.

This section is outlined as follows.

•

Section
B.1
establishes the landscape of the unpenalized NegDRO in both the population and finite-sample regimes.

•

Section
B.2
presents the proof of Proposition
2
, which relies on the landscape of unpenalized NegDRO.

•

Lastly, we propose the algorithm to identify the stationary points for NegDRO in Section
B.3
along with its convergence guarantees towards the causal outcome model.

B.1
Landscape of Unpenalized NegDRO

In this section, we shall establish the benign landscapes for unpenalized NegDRO, for both population and empirical versions, parallel to Theorem
4
in the main article.

The following theorem presents the landscape of unpenalized population NegDRO, where we control the distance between any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
, by leveraging the distance between subdifferential
\partial\Phi(b)
to the origin. The proof is provided in Section
D.3
.

Theorem 8

(Landscape of Unpenalized Population NegDRO)
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose that Condition
1b
holds. The following inequality holds for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\displaystyle\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma{|\mathcal{E}|}}+{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))\right),

where
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))=\inf\{\|\zeta\|_{2},~\zeta\in\partial\Phi(b)\}
measures the distance of the subdifferential
\partial\Phi(b)
to the origin, and
\lambda>0
denotes the degree of environmental heterogeneity as defined in Condition
1b
.

This bound closely aligns with the penalized case in Theorem
4
, except for two differences: the gradient norm is replaced with the subdifferential distance, and the term related to the ridge-penalty parameter
\mu
is removed. Moreover, this bound directly indicates that for sufficiently large
\gamma\geq 0
, any (generalized) stationary point of NegDRO with
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))=0
closely approximates the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
.

We next turn to the finite-sample regime, where we observe
\{x^{e}_{i},y^{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{e}}
for each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
. The following theorem establishes the landscape of empirical NegDRO, the proof of which is provided in Section
D.4
.

Theorem 9

(Landscape of Unpenalized Empirical NegDRO)
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Conditions
1b
,
2
hold and
n\geq(1\vee c_{1}/\lambda^{2})p
. Then, for any
0\leq s\leq(1\wedge c_{2}\lambda^{2})n-p
, with a probability of at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
, the following inequality holds uniformly for all predictors
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

\left\|b-\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))\right)+\frac{C}{\lambda^{1/2}}\left(\frac{p+s}{{n}}\right)^{1/4},

(51)

where
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))=\inf\{\|\zeta\|_{2},\zeta\in\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)\}
measures the distance of the subdifferential
\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)
to the origin,
\lambda>0
denotes the degree of environmental heterogeneity as defined in Condition
1b
, and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are absolute constants.

This theorem implies that if
b
is a (generalized) stationary point of the empirical objective function
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
, such that
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))=0
, it closely approximates the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
with an error
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}((\gamma|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}+n^{-1/4})
. Motivated by this insight, we present the algorithm designs to identify the (generalized) stationary points of
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
in Appendix
B.3
.

B.2
Proof of Proposition
2

For each fixed
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, the map

b\mapsto\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

is continuous in
b
. Since
\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
is compact, taking the maximization preserves continuity; that is,

\Phi(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

is proper and continuous in
b
.
By Fermat’s rule, every local minimizer
b
of
\Phi(\cdot)
satisfies the first-order necessary condition with
0\in\partial\Phi(b)
.

Since
b_{\rm Neg}^{\gamma}
is defined to be the global optimizer of
\Phi(\cdot)
, it follows
0\in\partial\Phi(b_{\rm Neg}^{\gamma})
with

{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b_{\rm Neg}^{\gamma}))=0.

Now we apply Theorem
8
to complete the proof.

B.3
Algorithm Design for Unpenalized NegDRO

Inspired by Theorem
9
, it suffices to compute a stationary point of
\widehat{\Phi}
to approximate
\beta^{*}
.
To compute the stationary points of the nonsmooth function
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
, we introduce a subgradient-based Algorithm
2
.
In contrast to Algorithm
1
in the main article, this new algorithm updates predictor
b^{t}
using
subgradient descent
in each iteration
t
, instead of gradient descent. Specifically, for each iteration, we first identify a maximizer weight vector
w_{t+1}
as shown in (
53
), and then update the predictor
b_{t+1}
using subgradient descent as in (
54
).

Moreover, this new algorithm employs a technique named proximal mapping to evaluate convergence,
where the proximal mapping is given by: for a parameter
\upsilon>0
,

{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)=\argmin_{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\widehat{\Phi}(\zeta)+\frac{1}{2\upsilon}\|\zeta-b\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(52)

We shall emphasize that while
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
itself is not convex, it can be shown to possess weak convexity, which implies that the optimization problem on the right-hand side of (
52
) is strongly convex towards
\zeta
, for a sufficiently small
\upsilon
; thus, it is computationally efficient to solve the proximal mapping. We provide a formal definition of weak convexity and the detailed arguments in Appendix
D.5
.

Algorithm 2

Subgradient Descent-Maximization Algorithm for the Original NegDRO in (
50
)

Input :
The number of iterations
T
, step size
\alpha
, initial point
b_{0}
, regularization parameter
\gamma\geq 0
and proximal-mapping parameter
\upsilon>0

Output :

\hat{b}^{\gamma}

for

t=0,1,...,T-1

do

Update the maximizer weight associated with
b_{t}
:

\displaystyle w_{t+1}\in\argmax_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left({w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-(b_{t})^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2};

(53)

Update
b^{t+1}
corresponding to the weight
w_{t+1}
:

b_{t+1}=b_{t}-\alpha\cdot\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left([{w}_{t+1}]_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\nabla\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-(b_{t})^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2};

(54)

Compute proximal mapping point
{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t+1})
as in (
52
).

end for

Define
\hat{b}^{\gamma}\in\argmin_{\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}}\|{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})-b_{t}\|_{2}
.

We now provide additional discussions on the usage of proximal mapping, which is utilized in the final output selection step of the current Algorithm
2
. Previously, for the ridge-penalized NegDRO, which is differentiable, Algorithm
1
selects the output by minimizing the gradient norm
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}
. This ensures that the selected
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
approaches a stationary point of smooth
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\cdot)
.
In contrast, for the unpenalized NegDRO, which is not differentiable everywhere, we aim to minimize the subdifferential distances to the origin,
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b_{t}))
, for
t=1,...,T
, as implied by Theorem
9
. However, the quantities
\{{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b_{t}))\}_{t}
are not directly observable. To address this issue, the current Algorithm
2
employs proximal mapping by selecting the smallest
\|{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})-b_{t}\|_{2}
. In Appendix
D.5
, we shall show that this quantity upper bounds the
\min_{t}{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b_{t}))
, thereby effectively approaching a stationary point for the nonsmooth
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
.

We impose a standard regularity condition on the iterates produced by algorithm
2
, which is adapted from Condition
3
in the main text.

Condition 5

(Gradient Boundedness)
.

All iterates
\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}
produced by Algorithm
2
remain within a bounded set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{e\in\mathcal{E},b\in\mathcal{B}}\|\nabla\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}\|_{2}\leq C<\infty.

(55)

We now analyze the output of Algorithm
2
by establishing its convergence rate to
\beta^{*}
, with its proof provided in Section
D.5
.

Theorem 10
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose that Conditions
1b
,
2
,
5
hold, and
the sample size satisfies
n\geq(1\vee c_{1}/\lambda^{2})p
.
Set the step size to
\alpha\asymp T^{-1/2}
. Then for any
0\leq s\leq(1\wedge c_{2}\lambda^{2})n-p
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
,
the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
of Algorithm
2
satisfies:

\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+T^{-1/4}\right)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\left(\frac{p+s}{{n}}\right)^{1/4},

where
\lambda
denotes the degree of environmental heterogeneity as defined in Condition
1b
, and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are absolute constants.

The above theorem shows that the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
approximates
\beta^{*}
up to an error
\mathcal{O}((\gamma|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}+T^{-1/4}+n^{-1/4})
, aligned with the penalized case in Theorem
5
when the ridge-penalty parameter
\mu
is set to
cM/\sqrt{T}
.
The convergence rates with respect to both the sample size n and iteration times
T
are consistent with those discussed for Theorem
5
.

We further discuss the rate
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/4})
in the preceding theorem.
The derived convergence rate for
\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
is obtained by leveraging the upper bound of
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
for any prediction model
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
as established in Theorem
9
, in conjunction with identifying a nearly (generalized) stationary point
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
such that
{\rm Dist}(0,\widehat{\Phi}(b))
is sufficiently small. Notably, finding generalized stationary points for non-smooth functions, such as our NegDRO objective
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
, inherently requires a
T^{-1/4}
convergence rate
[
Davis and Drusvyatskiy, 2018a
]
.
While this generalized stationary convergence rate is considered optimal without additional structural assumptions, it raises the intriguing possibility of directly quantifying
\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
without relying on stationary convergence theory, which could potentially lead to improved rates or new insights.

Appendix C
Additional Results on the Role of the Optimization Objective

In Section
C.1
, we establish the benign landscape of the unpenalized NegDRO under the limited additive intervention regime.

Building upon that result, this section extends the discussion to the general additive intervention regime and emphasizes the importance of the optimization objective in NegDRO.
Specifically, Section
C.2
introduces a relaxed identification condition for the general additive intervention regime.

Furthermore, Section
C.3
provides additional background and methodological comparisons, highlighting how alternative causal invariance learning frameworks, Causal Dantzig and DRIG, address additive interventions using different formulations of the underlying optimization problem.

C.1
Benign Landscape for the Limited Additive Intervention Regime

We define the following quantities: with the specific weight
w^{0}
from Condition
4
,

\lambda_{1}:=\lambda_{\rm min}\left([(\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})]_{S_{L},S_{L}}\right),\quad\lambda_{2}:=\lambda_{\rm min}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}_{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right).

The following theorem establishes the benign landscape of the unpenalized NegDRO objective under the limited additive intervention regime.
The detailed proof is provided in Appendix
D.6
.

Theorem 11

(Benign Landscape of Unpenalized NegDRO)
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose there is no hidden confounding between the outcome and covariates, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=0
. If Condition
4
holds, then the following inequality holds for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\kappa{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))+\kappa^{1/2}({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b)))^{1/2}+\kappa^{1/4}({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b)))^{1/4}+\kappa^{-1/2},

where
\kappa=1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|
, and
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))=\inf\{\|\zeta\|_{2},~\zeta\in\partial\Phi(b)\}
measures the distance of the subdifferential
\partial\Phi(b)
to the origin.

Compared with Theorem
8
, which characterizes the full additive intervention regime, Theorem
11
shows that even under the more challenging limited interventions, the NegDRO landscape remains benign.

In particular, if a stationary point of
\Phi(b)
is reached such that
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))=0
, then it approximates
\beta^{*}
, then this stationary point is guaranteed to be close to
\beta^{*}
with error bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left((1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1/2}\right)
. This convergence rate is slower than the
\mathcal{O}\left((1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}\right)
rate in Theorem
8
for the full intervention regime.

In practice, however, optimization algorithms like Algorithm
2
rarely reach an exact stationary point. Suppose the algorithm attains an
\epsilon
-stationary solution satisfying
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))\leq\epsilon
. Then Theorem
11
implies that

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\kappa\epsilon+\kappa^{1/2}\epsilon^{1/2}+\kappa^{1/4}\epsilon^{1/4}+\kappa^{-1/2}.

To ensure that
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
converges to zero, it is necessary that
\epsilon\ll(1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}
and that
\gamma\to\infty
. This requirement is more stringent than in the full intervention regime, where Theorem
8
only requires
\epsilon\to 0
and
\gamma\to 0
for consistency. Therefore, the limited intervention setting demands higher algorithmic precision, as the algorithm must locate a more accurate stationary point to ensure the resulting predictor remains close to
\beta^{*}
.

For brevity, we omit the discussion of the empirical benign landscape under the limited intervention regime. The empirical landscape bound follows essentially a similar structure to Theorem
11
, up to additional finite-sample errors.

C.2
Relaxed Identification Conditions for General Additive Interventions

Section
5
in the main text introduces a relaxed identification condition tailored to the limited additive intervention regime, where only a subset of covariates is subject to interventions.
Here, we extend this idea and propose a more general relaxed condition that applies to the general additive intervention regime, encompassing the limited intervention case as a special instance.

Recall the definition of
\mathbf{\Delta}(w)
in (
30
): for any weight vector
w\in\Delta^{L}
,

\mathbf{\Delta}(w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}].

We now introduce the following identification condition, which involves the vector
h
and matrix
\mathbf{H}\succeq 0
defined in Lemma
1
.

Condition 6
.

There exists some weight
w^{0}\in\Delta^{L}
such that both of the following conditions hold:

(i)

The matrix
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})
satisfies that

\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succeq 0\quad\textrm{and}~~\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succ-{\mathbf{H}}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}],

(56)

(ii)

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})=V^{0}\Lambda^{0}(V^{0})^{\intercal}
denote its spectral decomposition, where
\Lambda^{0}={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1}^{0}\cdots\lambda_{p}^{0})
contains eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}^{0}\geq 0
for each
j\in[p]
, and
V^{0}=(v_{1}^{0}\cdots v_{p}^{0})
are the corresponding eigenvectors. The vector
h
lies in the subspace spanned by the eigenvectors associated with positive eigenvalues:

h\in{\rm Span}(\{v_{j}:~~\textrm{for $j\in[p]$ satisfies }\lambda_{j}^{0}>0\}).

(57)

Condition
6
relaxes the earlier Condition
1b
, which requires strict positive definiteness.
Here, we only require that
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})
be positive semidefinite and that the vector
h
lies within the subspace spanned by its strictly positive eigenvectors.
To see that this condition is a strict relaxation of the earlier Condition
1b
, note from Lemma
1
that

\bm{A}(w^{0})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\mathbf{G},

where
\mathbf{G}
is a full-rank matrix. Thus, if Condition
1b
holds with
\bm{A}(w^{0})\succ 0
, it follows that
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succ 0
, and hence (
56
) is automatically satisfied.
Moreover, since all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})
are strictly positive, we have

\mathrm{Span}\left(\{v_{j}^{0}:\lambda_{j}^{0}>0\}\right)=\mathbb{R}^{p},

so that (
57
) also holds.
Therefore, Condition
6
strictly relaxes Condition
1b
.

The following theorem shows that the relaxed Condition
6
suffices for causal identification, i.e., the optimal solution of the constrained problem (
7
) is
\beta^{*}
. The proof of Theorem
12
is given in Appendix
D.7
.

Theorem 12
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Condition
6
holds. Then the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
achieves the smallest prediction risk among all risk-invariant predictors. Consequently, the proposed constrained problem (
7
) exactly recovers
\beta^{*}
.

C.3
Discussions of Existing Approaches

In this subsection, we will provide the detailed analysis regarding the existing approaches tailored for the additive intervention regime: CausalDantzig
[
Meinshausen, 2018
]
and DRIG
[
Shen et al., 2025
]
.

We start with the analysis of Causal Dantzig, where they consider two observed environments
e
and
f
, with samples
\{x^{e}_{i},y^{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{e}}
and
\{x^{f}_{i},y^{f}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{f}}
. They define

\widehat{\mathbf{G}}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}x^{e\intercal}_{i}-\frac{1}{n_{f}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{f}}x^{f}_{i}x^{f\intercal}_{i},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}y^{e}_{i}-\frac{1}{n_{f}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{f}}x^{f}_{i}y^{f}_{i}.

If
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
is invertible, the causal dantzig estimator would be

\hat{\beta}=\widehat{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}.

When
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
is not invertible, as in the case where
p\gg n
, they proposed the regularized causal Dantzig estimator as the solution to the following optimization problem:

\hat{\beta}^{\lambda}=\argmin\|\beta\|_{1}\quad\textrm{s.t.}~~\|\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}-\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\beta\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda,

where
\lambda>0
is the tuning parameter. Suppose that
\mathbf{G}=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbf{G}}]
is invertible and other regular conditions hold. let the tuning parameter
\lambda\asymp\sqrt{\log(p)/(n_{1}\wedge n_{2})}\to 0
,
Meinshausen [2018, Section 4]
establishes that with high probability,

\|\hat{\beta}^{\lambda}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(p)}{n_{1}\wedge n_{2}}}.

Now, we will move on discussing the DRIG method
[
Shen et al., 2025
]
, where they assume the existence and availability of a reference environment, denoted as
e=0
. And all other environments
\mathcal{E}=\{1,2,...,|\mathcal{E}|\}
have stronger interventions than the reference environment with

\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{0}}{\delta^{0\intercal}}]\prec\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\textrm{ for any environment $e\in\mathcal{E}$.}

The DRIG estimator of the population version is the solution to the following optimization problem:

b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma}=\argmin_{b}\mathbb{E}[\ell(X^{0},Y^{0};b)]+\gamma\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\left(\mathbb{E}[\ell(X^{e},Y^{e};b)-\mathbb{E}[\ell(X^{0},Y^{0};b)]\right),

where
\gamma
is the regularization parameter,
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
is some pre-fix weight.
In comparison with DRIG, our proposed NegDRO as defined in (
9
) does not assume the existence or availability of such a reference environment
e=0
, and we do not need to specify a weight
w
in prior.

It can be shown that if
e=0
is indeed a reference environment,
b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma}
will be the unique stationary point of their optimization problem. Therefore, they solve
b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma}
by setting the gradient being zero:

(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}\left[X^{0}(Y^{0}-(b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma})^{\intercal}X^{0})\right]+\gamma\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}\left[X^{e}(Y^{e}-(b_{\rm DRIG}^{\gamma})^{\intercal}X^{e})\right]=0.

When we set
\gamma
to
\infty
, the above equation becomes

\mathbb{E}\left[X^{0}(Y^{0}-(b_{\rm DRIG}^{\infty})^{\intercal}X^{0})\right]=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}\left[X^{e}(Y^{e}-(b_{\rm DRIG}^{\infty})^{\intercal}X^{e})\right].

By organizing the above equation and assuming that
\mathbb{E}[X^{0}X^{(0)\intercal}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]
is invertible, we establish that

b_{\rm DRIG}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{0}X^{(0)\intercal}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{0}Y^{(0)}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}Y^{e}]\right).

Since the causal outcome model
\beta^{*}
satisfies (
31
), it holds that

\beta^{*}=\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{0}X^{(0)\intercal}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{0}Y^{(0)}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}Y^{e}]\right).

Combining the above two equations, we shall show that

b_{\rm DRIG}^{\infty}=\beta^{*},

if
\mathbb{E}[X^{0}X^{(0)\intercal}]-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{e}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]
is invertible with the prespecified weight
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}.

However when there does not exist such a reference environment or the matrix is non-invertible, provided that some covariates are not intervened for all environments, the DRIG method fails to achieve causal identification.

To conclude, both CausalDantzig and DRIG rely on the invertibility of the difference of gram matrices for causal identification, which can be violated if some covariates have not been intervened in all environments
e\in\mathcal{E}.

Appendix D
Proofs for Other Results

D.1
Proof of Theorem
7

It follows from Lemma
2
that the weighted empirical risk can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

(58)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[(\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\hat{r}^{e})+2(h-\hat{q}^{e})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left(\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]+\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}\right)(b-\beta^{*})\right]
