\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Now we study the expression of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
defined in (
22
). With slight abuse of notation, we define

\bar{w}=\argmax_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\left\{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(59)

Together with (
58
), we establish that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

(60)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}.

Step-1:

Express
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
through its gradient
\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
.

The gradient
\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
is given by:

\displaystyle\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h+2\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+2\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[-2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{q}^{e}+2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Left multiplying the vector
\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})
, we establish that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Combining the above equality with (
44
), we establish that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\Phi_{\mu}(b)+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}

(61)

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Step-2:

Establish the lower bound of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
via the weight
w^{0}
specified in Condition
1b
.

Since
\bar{w}
is the maximizer weight as defined in (
59
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w^{0})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succeq 0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, and
\bm{A}(w^{0})\succeq\lambda\mathbf{I}
as specified in Condition
1b
, we further establish that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}-\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

(62)

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

Step-3:

Combine the results in the steps 1 and 2. It follows from (
61
) and (
62
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)-\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}-\mu\|\bar{w}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle+\mu\|w^{0}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

By Chebyshev’s inequality and the fact that
\bar{w},w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, we establish that

\displaystyle\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\mu+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

We further apply the results in Lemma
3
and establish that for any
s\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+3\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|\hat{q}^{e}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+2\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}|\hat{r}^{e}|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}+\frac{p+s}{n}\right)(\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+1).

With
n\geq p+s
, we combine the above two inequalities and obtain

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\mu+C\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}(\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+1),

for some constant
C>0
.
When
\lambda\geq 2C\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}
,
the above inequality further implies that

\frac{1}{2}\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+C\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\mu+C\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}.

According to Lemma
1
,
\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}
and
\|h\|_{2}
are upper bounded by some constants; it holds that

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+C\left(\mu+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right).

Therefore, there exist constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that if
n\geq(1\vee c_{0}/\lambda^{2})p
, then for any value
0\leq s\leq(1\wedge c_{1}\lambda^{2})n-p
, the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
:

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left[\sqrt{\mu}+\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}\right].

Since we consider
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}
, which implies that

\frac{1}{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4},

we shall further obtain that

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\left\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left[\sqrt{\mu}+\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}\right].

The proof completes.

D.2
Proof of Proposition
3

Recall that in (
22
), we define

\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{L}}\left\{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

We first introduce the following lemma, which establishes the smoothness property of the inner objective function of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
.
The proof is provided in Appendix
E.4
.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose Condition
3
holds. Then the function

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}

is
(2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu)
-smooth in the joint variable
(b,w)
.

We next utilize the following lemma to establish the smoothness of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
. This lemma essentially adapts from Lemma 4.3 of
Lin et al. [2020a]
. For completeness, we provide its proof in Appendix
E.5
.

Lemma 5

(Lemma 4.3
[
Lin et al., 2020a
]
)
.

Suppose the function
f(x,y)
is

•

\ell
-smooth in the joint variable
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
such that

\|\nabla f(x_{1},y_{1})-\nabla f(x_{2},y_{2})\|_{2}\leq\ell\sqrt{\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{2}^{2}},

•

\mu
-strongly concave in
y\in\mathcal{Y}
for every fixed
x
, and

•

the feasible set
\mathcal{Y}
is convex and bounded.

Then the function
\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(x,y)
is
(\ell+\ell^{2}/\mu)
-smooth in
x
.

From Lemma
4
, we have established that the function

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}

is
(2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu)
-smooth in the joint variable
(b,w)
. Moreover, the term
-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}
ensures that this function is
2\mu
-strongly concave in
w\in\Delta^{L}
.
Applying Lemma
5
, we obtain that
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
is
L_{\Phi}
-smooth in
b
, with

L_{\widehat{\Phi}}=2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu+\frac{2(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+\mu)^{2}}{\mu}.

(63)

Given the smoothness of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
, the following inequality holds for any
b_{1},b_{2}
:

\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{2})\leq\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{1})+[\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{1})]^{\intercal}(b_{2}-b_{1})+\frac{L_{\widehat{\Phi}}}{2}\|b_{2}-b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}.

(64)

Algorithm
1
performs a gradient descent step on
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)
with step size

\alpha=\frac{1}{L_{\widehat{\Phi}}},\hskip 9.24994ptb_{t+1}=b_{t}-\alpha\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t}).

Substituting
b_{1}=b_{t}
and
b_{2}=b_{t+1}
into (
64
) gives

\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t+1})\leq\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})-\frac{1}{2L_{\widehat{\Phi}}}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}.

This inequality shows that each gradient step strictly decreases the objective unless the gradient vanishes.

Summing the above inequality over
t=0,...,T
yields

\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{T+1})-\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{0})\leq-\frac{1}{2L_{\widehat{\Phi}}}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b)\geq-\mu
for all
b
, the left-hand side is bounded below by
-\mu-\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{0})
. Hence, we have

\frac{1}{T+1}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2L_{\widehat{\Phi}}(\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{0})+\mu)}{T+1}.

Recall that the output of the algorithm selects the iterate with the smallest gradient norm, that is,

\hat{b}^{\gamma}:=\argmin_{\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2},

Then,

\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{T+1}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2L_{\widehat{\Phi}}(\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{0})+\mu)}{T+1}

Lastly, we simplify the results mentioned above. Let
A:=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}
. Then it follows from (
63
) that

L_{\widehat{\Phi}}=2(A+\mu)+\frac{2(A+\mu)^{2}}{\mu}=2\left(\frac{A^{2}}{\mu}+3A+2\mu\right).

By treating
A
as a constant, we have

\frac{A^{2}}{\mu}\lesssim L_{\widehat{\Phi}}\lesssim\frac{A^{2}}{\mu}+A+\mu\asymp\frac{A^{2}}{\mu}+\mu.

And if
\mu\lesssim\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{0})
, the output gradient norm satisfies that

\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{T+1}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\mu}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{A^{2}}{\mu}+\mu\right)\asymp\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}|\mathcal{E}|}{\mu}+\mu\right).

D.3
Proof of Theorem
8

For convenience, for a predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and weight
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, we define

{\phi}(b,w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right){\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}.

(65)

By the definition of
{\Phi}(b)
in (
17
), we have

{\Phi}(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}{\phi}(b,w).

Given a predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we define the set
\mathcal{W}(b)
of maximizer weights such that:

\mathcal{W}(b):=\argmax_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}{\phi}(b,w).

(66)

Since
{\phi}(b,w)
is continuous towards
w
for every
b
, and
\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
is a compact set, the set
\mathcal{W}(b)
is non-empty by Weierstrass’ Theorem.

Step-1:
Express
{\Phi}(b)
through the partial derivative
\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}
for any
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
.

It follows from (
43
) that

\displaystyle{\phi}(b,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*}).

Then for any maximizer weight
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
, we study the partial derivative of
{\phi}(b,\bar{w})
as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h+2\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+2\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*}).

Left multiplying the vector
\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})
, we establish that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\Phi}(b)={\phi}(b,\bar{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}.

(67)

Step-2:
Establish the lower bound of
{\Phi}(b)
via the weight
w^{0}
specified in Condition
1b
.

By the definition of
\bar{w}
, we have

\displaystyle{\Phi}(b)

\displaystyle=\phi(b,\bar{w})\geq{\phi}(b,w^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w^{0})(b-\beta^{*}).

Since
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succeq 0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, and
\bm{A}(w^{0})\succeq\lambda\mathbf{I}
as specified in Condition
1b
, we further establish that

\displaystyle{\Phi}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

(68)

Step-3:
Combine the results in the steps 1 and 2. It follows from (
67
) and (
68
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}-\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

By Chebyshev’s inequality and the fact that
\bar{w},w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, we establish that

\displaystyle\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

According to Lemma
1
that
\mathbf{G}
is a constant full-rank matrix and
\|h\|\leq c\sigma_{Y}^{2}
for some constant
c>0
, it holds that

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}.

Step-4:
Upper bound
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
with subdifferential’s distance
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial{\Phi}(b)).

We know that the preceding inequality holds for any
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
, where
\mathcal{W}(b)
is defined in (
66
). Then the following inequality holds:

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\inf_{{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right).

We next introduce the lemma to facilitate our control of the term
\inf_{{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}
. This lemma essentially follows Danskin’s theorem
[
Danskin, 1966
]
[
Bertsekas, 1997
, Proposition B.25]
, and its proof is provided in Section
E.6
.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
f(x,z)
is a continuous function of two arguments,
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is a compact set. Define

F(x)=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x,z),
and the set of maximizing points
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)
as:

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)=\left\{\bar{z}:~~f(x,\bar{z})=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x,z)\right\}
.
If
f(x,z)
is differentiable with respect to
x
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, and if
\partial f/\partial x
is continuous with respect to
z
for all
x
, then the subdifferential of
F(x)
is given by

\partial F(x)={\rm conv}\left\{\frac{\partial f(x,z)}{\partial x}:~~z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)\right\},

where
{\rm conv}
indicates the convex hull operation. Moreover, if
\mathcal{Z}
is a convex set and
f(x,z)
is a linear function with respect to
z
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the
{\rm conv}
operation can be removed, and it holds that

\partial F(x)=\left\{\frac{\partial f(x,z)}{\partial x}:~~z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)\right\}.

We shall verify the conditions of the above lemma hold for our
\phi(b,w)
with
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
w\in\Delta^{L}
. Then we apply this lemma to obtain that:

\partial{\Phi}(b)=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial b}{\phi}(b,\bar{w}),~~\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)\right\}.

Therefore, it holds that

{\rm Dist}(0,\partial{\Phi}(b))=\inf_{\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial b}{\phi}(b,\bar{w})\right\|_{2}
, and

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left({\rm Dist}(0,\partial{\Phi}(b))+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right).

D.4
Proof of Theorem
9

The proof of this theorem essentially follows that of Theorem
8
, with the additional work of controlling finite-sample error terms.

For convenience, for a predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and weight
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, we define

\hat{\phi}(b,w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}.

(69)

By the definition of
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
in (
50
), we have

\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\hat{\phi}(b,w).

Given a predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we define the set
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
of maximizer weights such that:

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b):=\argmax_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\hat{\phi}(b,w).

(70)

Since
\hat{\phi}(b,w)
is continuous towards
w
for every
b
, and
\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
is a compact set, the set
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
is non-empty by Weierstrass’ Theorem.

Step-1:
Express
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
through the partial derivative
\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}
for any
\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
.

It follows from (
58
) that

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}(b,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w)(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Then for any maximizer weight
\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
, we study the partial derivative of
\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})
as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h+2\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+2\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[-2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{q}^{e}+2\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Left multiplying the vector
\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})
, we establish that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(\bar{w})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

(71)

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Step-2:
Establish the lower bound of
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
via the weight
w^{0}
specified in Condition
1b
.

By the definition of
\bar{w}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}(b)

\displaystyle=\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})\geq\hat{\phi}(b,w^{0})

(72)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right](b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w^{0})(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right].

Since
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succeq 0
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, and
\bm{A}(w^{0})\succeq\lambda\mathbf{I}
as specified in Condition
1b
, we further establish that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

(73)

\displaystyle+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

Step-3:
Combine the results in the steps 1 and 2. It follows from (
71
) and (
73
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}-\frac{h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

By Chebyshev’s inequality and the fact that
\bar{w},w^{0}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, we establish that

\displaystyle\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

We further apply the results in Lemma
3
to control the components involving the finite-sample error terms
\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e},\hat{q}^{e},\hat{r}^{e}
on the left-hand side of the above inequality. We establish that for any
s\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\bar{w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

(74)

\displaystyle-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w^{0}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left[\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|\hat{\mathbf{P}}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\|_{2}^{2}+3\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|\hat{q}^{e}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\|_{2}+2\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}|\hat{r}^{e}|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}+\frac{p+s}{n}\right)(\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+1).

With
n\geq p+s
, we combine the above two inequalities and obtain

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\left\|\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\|h\|_{2}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}(\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+1)

When
\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\lesssim\lambda
, together with Lemma
1
which indicates that
\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}
and
\|h\|_{2}
are bounded, the above inequality further implies that

\lambda\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left(\left\|\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}

Then we show that

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\left\|\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}.

(75)

Step-4:
Upper bound
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
with subdifferential’s distance
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)).

We know that the above inequality (
75
) holds for any
\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)
is defined in (
70
). Then the following inequality holds:

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\inf_{\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}.

Now we apply Lemma
6
to obtain that:

\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w}),~~\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)\right\}.

Therefore, it holds that

{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))=\inf_{\bar{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\hat{\phi}(b,\bar{w})\right\|_{2}
, and

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}.

Since we consider
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{p+s}{n}}
, the above inequality shall be simplified as:

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b))+\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}.

D.5
Proof of Theorem
10

We first introduce the concept of weak convexity. While
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
itself is not convex, it can be shown to possess weak convexity, which generalizes the idea of convexity by allowing for a certain controlled degree of curvature. This distinction is crucial, as weak convexity provides a meaningful balance between convex and highly non-convex behavior, enabling the development of more robust optimization techniques
[
Poliquin and Rockafellar, 1992
,
Poliquin and Rockafellar, 1996
]
.

Definition 5

(Weak convexity)
.

The function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\rho
-weakly convex if
f(\cdot)+\frac{\rho}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2}
is convex.

The following Lemma demonstrates that
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
is weakly convex with high probability, whose proof is provided in Appendix
E.7
.

Lemma 7
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Condition
2
holds, and the sample size satisfies
n\geq p
. Then there exists a positive constant
\rho>0
such that for any
s\in[0,n-p]
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
, the function
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
is
\rho
-weakly convex.

After we generalized the convexity concept for
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
, now we will move on discussing the challenge arising from the non-smoothness landscape of
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
. To facilitate discussion, we present the definition of nearly stationary points as follows.

Definition 6

(Nearly Stationary Point)
.

A point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is an
\epsilon
-stationary point of the function
h:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
for some tolerance
\epsilon\geq 0
if
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial h(b)):=\inf\{\|\zeta\|^{2}_{2}:\zeta\in\partial h(b)\}\leq\epsilon
, where
\partial h(b)
is the subdifferential defined in Definition
3
. We call a point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
a nearly stationary point if the tolerance
\epsilon
is small enough.

Identifying nearly stationary points, for non-smooth functions, like
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
, is particularly challenging due to the sharp transitions in the function’s sub-gradient. In smooth optimization, the gradient provides a clear, continuous direction toward stationary points, making it straightforward to determine whether a point is close enough to being stationary. However, in non-smooth functions, there can be abrupt changes in the sub-gradient, and sub-gradient fails to provide insightful criterion on the closeness of a point to being stationary.
A simple example is
|x|
where the only stationary point is
0
, but any other point
x\neq 0
is not an
\epsilon
-stationary point with
\epsilon<1
, no matter how close
x
is to
0
.

To address this issue, we employ a smooth approximation technique using the Moreau envelope
[
Davis and Drusvyatskiy, 2018b
,
Davis and Drusvyatskiy, 2018a
,
Davis and Grimmer, 2019
,
Zhang and He, 2018
,
Rafique et al., 2022
]
. By smoothing
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
, we transform the non-smooth landscape into a differentiable one, allowing for a more gradual transition in the gradient. This smoother approximation enables algorithms to track
\epsilon
-stationary points more effectively.

We define the
Moreau envelope
of function
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
with the parameter
\upsilon>0
as follows:

\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)=\min_{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\widehat{\Phi}(\zeta)+\frac{1}{2\upsilon}\|\zeta-b\|^{2}\right\}.

(76)

We shall establish that the Moreau envelope
\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)
is differentiable with respect to
b
, with gradient denoted as
\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)
as long as the parameter
\upsilon
is small enough in the following lemma. The lemma’s proof is provided in Section
E.8
.

Lemma 8
.

If
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
is
\rho
-weakly convex, as long as
\upsilon<\rho^{-1}
, its Moreau envelope
\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)
, defined in (
76
), is differentiable with the gradient given by

\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)=\upsilon^{-1}\left(b-{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)\right),

where
{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)
is the proximal mapping defined as follows:

{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)=\argmin_{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\widehat{\Phi}(\zeta)+\frac{1}{2\upsilon}\|\zeta-b\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

Moreover, the proximal mapping
{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)
satisfies that

\|{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b)-b\|=\upsilon\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)\|,

(77)

and

{\rm Dist}\left(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}({\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b))\right)\leq\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(b)\|.

(78)

The results in (
77
) and (
78
) shed light on the effective identification of
\epsilon
-stationary points. Suppose that we have some point
\bar{b}
, on which the gradient of the Moreau envelope is small enough such that
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(\bar{b})\|\leq\epsilon
. The inequality (
78
) indicates that the proximal point
{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(\bar{b})
is a
\epsilon
-stationary point with
{\rm Dist}\left(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}({\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(b))\right)\leq\epsilon.
Moreover, (
77
) establishes that the point
\bar{b}
is
\upsilon\epsilon
-closed to the
\epsilon
-stationary
{\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(\bar{b})
. To sum up, a small gradient
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(\bar{b})\|
implies that the point
\bar{b}
is
close
to some
nearly stationary
point of the function
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
.

The following lemma shows that the subdifferential
\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)
is bounded, whose proof is provided in Section
E.9
. We emphasize that it is essential and commonly adopted to consider the bounded subdifferential when analyzing nonsmooth functions in the literature, such as those in
Davis and Drusvyatskiy [2018b]
,
Nemirovski et al. [2009]
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose Condition
5
holds. Then there exists a constant
L>0
such that

\sup\left\{\|\zeta\|_{2}^{2},~~\zeta\in\partial\widehat{\Phi}(b)\right\}\leq L^{2},~~\textrm{for all }b\in\mathcal{B},

(79)

where
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
is bounded set imposed in Condition
5
.

We now summarize the results we have established till now.
Under Condition
2
, we know that
\widehat{\Phi}(\cdot)
is
\rho
-weakly convex function with high probability by applying the result in Lemma
7
, for some
\rho>0
. Then we will follow Lemma
8
by setting the parameter
\upsilon=\frac{1}{2\rho}
in (
76
) to obtain a differentiable Moreau envelope of
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
. Together with the bounded subdifferential result in Lemma
9
, we will demonstrate the convergence of Algorithm
2
with vanishing norm of the Moreau envelope
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b)\|_{2}
.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
\widehat{\Phi}(b)
is
\rho
-weakly convex, and (
79
) holds. There exists some constant
R
such that
\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b_{0})\leq R
with the initial point
b_{0}
.
We set the step sizes of Algorithm
2
as

\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{R}{\rho L^{2}(T+1)}}
.
Then it holds that

\frac{1}{T+1}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4L\sqrt{\frac{\rho R}{T+1}}.

The proof of Lemma essentially follows from
Davis and Drusvyatskiy [2018b, Theorem 2.1]
. For completeness, we will provide its proof in Section
E.10
.
In Algorithm
2
, for each iteration
b_{t}
, we will compute its proximal mapping point with
\upsilon=1/(2\rho)
:

{\rm prox}_{1/(2\rho)\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})=\argmin_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\widehat{\Phi}(x)+\rho\|x-b_{t}\|^{2}\right\}.

And it follows from Lemma
8
that

\|{\rm prox}_{1/(2\rho)\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})-b_{t}\|=\frac{1}{2\rho}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b_{t})\|.

Since the algorithm output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
among
\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}
with the smallest
\|{\rm prox}_{1/(2\rho)\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})-b_{t}\|
such that

\hat{b}^{\gamma}\in\argmin_{\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}}\|{\rm prox}_{1/(2\rho)\widehat{\Phi}}(b_{t})-b_{t}\|,

it follows that
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
achieves the smallest gradient of the Moreau envelope:

\hat{b}^{\gamma}\in\argmin_{\{b_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b_{t})\|^{2}.

Applying the results in Lemma
10
, we shall establish that

\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{1}{T+1}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(b_{t})\|^{2}_{2}\leq 4L\sqrt{\frac{\rho R}{T+1}}.

(80)

Combining the above results in Lemmas
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
, together with (
80
), we have established the following proposition.

Proposition 4
.

Under the additive intervention regime, suppose Conditions
2
,
5
hold, and the sample size satisfies
n\geq p
. Then there exists a positive constant
\rho>0
such that: for any
s\in[0,n-p]
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-s}
, the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
of Algorithm
2
satisfies:

\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|^{2}\lesssim T^{-1/2}.

where we set
\upsilon=\frac{1}{2\rho}
and the step size of Algorithm
2
as
\alpha\asymp T^{-1/2}
.

For notation convenience, we denote the proximal mapping point of the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
as:

[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}={\rm prox}_{\upsilon\widehat{\Phi}}(\hat{b}^{\gamma}).

We apply the result (
77
) in Lemma
8
and Proposition
4
to establish that

\|[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}-\hat{b}^{\gamma}\|_{2}=\upsilon\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{1/(2\rho)}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}\lesssim T^{-1/4}.

By triangle inequality, the above inequality implies that the distance between the output
\hat{b}^{\gamma}
and the causal effect
\beta^{*}
is controlled by

\displaystyle\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}+[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\|\hat{b}^{\gamma}-[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}\|_{2}+\|[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}

(81)

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{-1/4}+\|[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}.

Next, we are going to control the remaining term
\|[\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
.

Applying (
78
) in Lemma
8
, it holds that

{\rm Dist}\left(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}\left([\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox}\right)\right)\leq\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\upsilon}(\hat{b}^{\gamma})\|_{2}\lesssim T^{-1/4}.

(82)

Put the above inequality to Theorem
9
, we establish that

\displaystyle\|(\hat{b}^{\gamma})_{\rm prox}-\beta^{*}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+{\rm Dist}\left(0,\partial\widehat{\Phi}([\hat{b}^{\gamma}]_{\rm prox})\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}

(83)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\frac{1}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+T^{-1/4}\right]+\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\left(\frac{p+s}{n}\right)^{1/4}

Combining (
81
) and (
83
), we have completed the proof of Theorem
10
.

D.6
Proof of Theorem
11

The key idea is essentially the same as the proof of Theorem
4
, where we relate the population objective
\Phi(b)
to its subgradient and then use structural properties of the additive intervention regimes to obtain the upper bounds on
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
.

Step-1: Expressing
\Phi(b)
in terms of its subgradient.

From Step 1 of the proof for Theorem
D.3
, recall that (
67
) shows that

\displaystyle{\Phi}(b)={\phi}(b,\bar{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}.

(84)

where
\bar{w}
is a maximizer weight vector with
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
defined in (
66
).

Step-2: Lower bounding
\Phi(b)
through the specific weight
w^{0}
.

Using the lower bound argument in Step 2 of the proof for Theorem
D.3
, and writing

\bm{A}(w^{0})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[\delta^{e}\delta^{e\intercal}]\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\mathbf{G},

we have for the specific weight vector
w^{0}
:

\displaystyle{\Phi}(b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}+2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}+\frac{\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{A}(w^{0})(b-\beta^{*}),

(85)

where the second term on the right-hand side arises from the condition that
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{e}^{0}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\succeq\lambda_{2}{\bf I}
.

Step-3: Restricting to limited-intervention coordinates.

In the limited intervention regime, it holds
\delta^{e}_{j}\equiv 0
for all
j\notin S_{L}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Thus,

\displaystyle{}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}^{\intercal}[\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})]_{S_{L},S_{L}}[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}

(86)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{1}\left\|[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}\right\|_{2}^{2},

Furthermore, from Lemma
1
,
h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}+\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}].
When
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=0
,
h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}
. Note that
\mathbf{B}_{YX}
captures the direct causal relationship from
Y
to
X
with
D={\rm supp}(\mathbf{B}_{YX})\subseteq S_{L}
, it holds

h^{\intercal}[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]=\sigma_{Y}^{2}[\mathbf{B}_{YX}]_{S_{L}}^{\intercal}[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}.

(87)

Step-4: Combining inequalities.

Substituting (
86
) and (
87
) into
(
84
)–(
85
), and applying Cauchy–Schwarz, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{Y}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{YX}\|_{2}\|[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}\|_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\left\|[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

For notation convenience, we denote

r=\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2},\hskip 9.24994ptx=\|[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}\|_{2},\hskip 9.24994ptg=\left\|\frac{\partial{\phi}(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2},\hskip 9.24994pt\kappa=1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|.

Then we simplify the preceding inequality as follows:

\frac{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}{\kappa}x^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}\lesssim rg+\frac{x}{\kappa}.

(88)

Step-5: Bounding
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
preliminarily.

We establish the following inequality:

x=\|[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{G}\|_{2}r\lesssim r.

Therefore, if we drop the first term
\frac{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}{\kappa}x^{2}
on the LHS of (
88
), we obtain

\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}\lesssim gr+\frac{1}{\kappa}r.

Diving both sides by
\lambda_{2}r/\kappa
gives

r\lesssim\frac{g}{\lambda_{2}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}.

(89)

Step-6: Bounding
[\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})]_{S_{L}}
.

We remove the
\frac{\lambda_{2}r^{2}}{\kappa}
term on the LHS of (
88
) to get the inequality:

\frac{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}{\kappa}x^{2}\lesssim rg+\frac{x}{\kappa}.

Substitute (
89
) back into the product
rg
:

rg\lesssim\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)g=\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}g.

Therefore,

\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)x^{2}\lesssim x+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g.

We establish that

x\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g\right)}.

(90)

Step-7: Bounding
\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
.

Go back to (
88
), but drop the nonnegative
x^{2}
term to get the following inequality:

\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}\lesssim rg+\frac{x}{\kappa}.

Applying Young’s inequality to the term
rg
: for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

rg\leq\frac{\varepsilon\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon\lambda_{2}}g^{2}.

Choosing
\varepsilon=1/2
, the preceding two inequalities imply

\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}\lesssim\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{x}{\kappa}.

Now substitute the bound (
90
) for
x
:

\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\kappa}r^{2}\lesssim\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)\kappa}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g\right)}.

Multiplying both sides by
\kappa/\lambda_{2}
gives:

r^{2}\lesssim\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\lambda_{2}^{2}}g^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}(\kappa-1)\kappa}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}g\right)}.

Taking square roots and using
\sqrt{u+v+w}\leq\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}+\sqrt{w}
, we get

r\lesssim\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}g+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}(\kappa-1)\kappa}}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}\right)^{1/4}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/4}g^{1/2}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/4}g^{1/4}.

(91)

Step-8: Bounding via
{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))
.

Now we apply Lemma
6
, which essentially follows the celebrated Danskin’s theorem
[
Danskin, 1966
]
[
Bertsekas, 1997
, Proposition B.25]
, to obtain that:

\partial{\Phi}(b)=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial b}{\phi}(b,\bar{w}),~~\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)\right\}.

Therefore, it holds that

{\rm Dist}(0,\partial{\Phi}(b))=\inf_{\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)}\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial b}{\phi}(b,\bar{w})\right\|_{2}
. We denote

D:={\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b)).

Note that (
91
) holds for
g=\left\|\frac{\partial\phi(b,\bar{w})}{\partial b}\right\|_{2}
for any
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
. Taking the infimum over
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}(b)
gives:

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}D+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}(\kappa-1)\kappa}}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\kappa-1)}\right)^{1/4}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/4}D^{1/2}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\lambda_{2}}\right)^{1/4}D^{1/4}.

We now simplify the dependence by treating
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
as fixed constants. Using the expressions of
r=\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
and
D={\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))
, we achieve

\|b-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\kappa{\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b))+\kappa^{1/2}({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b)))^{1/2}+\kappa^{1/4}({\rm Dist}(0,\partial\Phi(b)))^{1/4}+\kappa^{-1/2},

with
\kappa=1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|
.

D.7
Proof of Theorem
12

Recall the constrained problem in (
7
) as follows:

\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}-b^{\intercal}X^{1}]^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt{\rm s.t}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{f}-b^{\intercal}X^{f}]^{2}\;\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$}.

We obtain its equivalent formulation by leveraging Lemma
1
:

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}2h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{1}}{\delta^{1\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

(92)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$},

where the explicit expressions of
h
and
\mathbf{G}
are given in Lemma
1
.

The remaining proof proceeds as follows:

Step-1
:

Under the additive intervention, suppose Condition
6
holds. We show that for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
subject to the constraint within (
92
) satisfies that
h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0
.

Step-2
:

Based on Step-1, we simplify the objective function, and prove that solving the problem (
92
) uniquely identifies
\beta^{*}
.

We now detail the remaining proof.

Step-1:

We observe that for any predictor
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying the constraint in (
92
), it must satisfy that:

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall w\in\Delta^{L}.

Therefore, for the specific weight vector
w^{0}
in Condition
6
, it holds

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0.

(93)

Since we consider
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succeq 0
with spectral decomposition:

\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})=V^{0}\Lambda^{0}(V^{0})^{\intercal},

where
\Lambda^{0}={\rm diag}(\lambda^{0}_{1},\lambda^{0}_{2},...,\lambda^{0}_{p})
is the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues with
\Lambda^{0}\succeq 0
, and
V^{0}=(v_{1}^{0},v_{2}^{0},...,v_{p}^{0})
are columns of corresponding eigenvectors.
We let
S_{+}
represent the set of indices for positive eigenvalues:

S_{+}=\{i\in[p]:~~\lambda_{i}^{0}>0\}.

For any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying that
u^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})u=0
, we shall have

0=u^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})u=u^{\intercal}V^{0}\Lambda^{0}(V^{0})^{\intercal}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}^{0}[(v_{i}^{0})^{\intercal}u]^{2}=\sum_{i\in S_{+}}\lambda_{i}^{0}[(v_{i}^{0})^{\intercal}u]^{2},

where the last equality holds since
\lambda_{i}^{0}=0
for all
i\notin S_{+}.

Moreover, considering that
\lambda_{i}>0
for
i\in S_{+}
, we further have

(v_{i}^{0})^{\intercal}u=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{for $i\in S_{+}$}.

In Condition
6
, the vector
h
lies within the positive eigenspace of
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})
in the sense that

h\in{\rm Span}\left\{v_{i}^{0},~~i\in S_{+}\right\}.

Combining the two preceding results, we shall establish that if a vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying
u^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})u=0
, then

h^{\intercal}u=0.

Together with (
93
), we obtain that

h^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=0.

Step-2:
Based on Step-1, we shall remove the first-order term (w.r.t.
b
) in the objective function of the problem (
92
) as follows:

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{1}}{\delta^{1\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

(94)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$},

Due to the risk-invariant constraint and
w^{0}\in\Delta^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{1}}{\delta^{1\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w^{0}_{e}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}).

Then we shall further write the optimization problem (
94
) equivalently:

\displaystyle\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left[\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})=(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{f}}{\delta^{f\intercal}}]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}),~~\textrm{for all $e,f\in\mathcal{E}$}.

We observe that
b=\beta^{*}
is a feasible point to the above optimization problem with the objective value
0
. And for any other feasible points
b\neq\beta^{*}
, the corresponding objective value satisfies that

(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left[\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right]\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})>0,

as we consider
\mathbf{\Delta}(w^{0})\succ-\mathbf{H}-\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]
as specified in Condition
6
. Consequently, we show that solving (
94
) exactly recovers
\beta^{*}
. Equivalently, the optimal solution of the constrained problem (
7
) identifies
\beta^{*}
.

Appendix E
Proof of Technical Lemmas

E.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Since
Y^{e}=(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}X^{e}+\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}
with the noise term satisfying
\mathbb{E}[(\varepsilon^{e})^{2}]=\sigma_{Y}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\beta^{*}-b)^{\intercal}X^{e}+\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}]^{2}

(95)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{Y}^{2}-2(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}](b-\beta^{*}).

Now, we continue to express
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]
and
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]
. It follows from (
11
) and (
12
)

X^{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,\cdot}[\eta+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
{\delta^{e}}\end{pmatrix}],\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\eta_{Y}.

Moreover, as we consider that
\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}\eta^{\intercal}]=0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]

\displaystyle=[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,\cdot}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta],

(96)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]

\displaystyle=[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,\cdot}\mathbb{E}[\eta\eta^{\intercal}][(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,\cdot}^{\intercal}+[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,-1}\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}][(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,-1}^{\intercal}.

Next, we study the term
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}
. It follows from (
10
) that

\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\\
-\mathbf{B}_{YX}&\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX}\end{pmatrix}

By the block matrix inversion formula, we establish that

(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1+(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{YX}&(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}\\
-\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{YX}&\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

We denote
\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}=\left[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}_{XX})-\mathbf{B}_{YX}(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}
, then

[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,\cdot}=(-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}_{YX},\mathbf{G}^{\intercal})\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt[(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}]_{-1,-1}=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}.

(97)

Due to the invertibility of
\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B}
, we know
\mathbf{G}
is a non-singular matrix as well.
Putting the above expressions back to (
96
), together with the notation that
\eta=(\eta_{Y},\eta_{X})^{\intercal}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}X^{e}]

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}_{YX}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{Y}]+\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}(-\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}+\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}])

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}-2\mathbf{B}_{YX}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]+\mathbb{E}[\eta_{X}\eta_{X}^{\intercal}]+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}.

We define

h=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}-\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}],

and

\mathbf{H}=\sigma_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{YX}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}-2\mathbf{B}_{YX}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{Y}\eta_{X}]+\mathbb{E}[\eta_{X}\eta_{X}^{\intercal}]=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\eta\eta^{\intercal}]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

We then establish that

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]=-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}h,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}.\end{gathered}

Putting the above expressions to (
95
), we complete the proof.

E.2
Proof of Lemma
2

According to SEMs in (
10
), we have

\begin{pmatrix}y^{e}_{i}\\
x^{e}_{i}\end{pmatrix}=(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{B})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}\\
\epsilon^{e}_{X,i}\end{pmatrix},

(98)

where

\{\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i},\epsilon^{e}_{X,i}\}_{i=1}^{n_{e}}
are the (unobserved) noise samples drawn from the distribution of additive noise terms
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},\varepsilon^{e}_{X})
.
Since
y^{e}_{i}=(\beta^{*})^{\intercal}x^{e}_{i}+\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}
, we shall express the empirical risk as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[y^{e}_{i}-b^{\intercal}x^{e}_{i}]^{2}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[(\beta^{*}-b)^{\intercal}x^{e}_{i}+\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}]^{2}-2(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}x^{e}_{i}+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}x^{e\intercal}_{i}(b-\beta^{*}).

Compared with the population risk
{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}
, whose expression presented in Lemma
1
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}-{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}

(99)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y})^{2}]\right)-2(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left(\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}x^{e}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\varepsilon^{e}_{X}]\right)

\displaystyle+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\left(\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}x^{e\intercal}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]\right)(b-\beta^{*}).

We continue investigating the three components on the right-hand side of the above equation.

It follows from (
11
) and (
97
) that

x^{e}_{i}=(-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}_{YX},\mathbf{G}^{\intercal})\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon_{Y,i}^{e}\\
\epsilon_{X,i}^{e}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994ptX^{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(-\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}_{YX},\mathbf{G}^{\intercal})\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{Y}^{e}\\
\varepsilon_{X}^{e}\end{pmatrix}.

We define the matrix
\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}
as follows:

\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}=\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}\\
\epsilon^{e}_{X,i}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}\\
\epsilon^{e}_{X,i}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\right].

(100)

Then it holds that:

\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}[\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y})^{2}]=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}\epsilon^{e}_{Y,i}x^{e}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}X^{e}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}x^{e\intercal}_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}X^{e\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}.

We define

\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix},~~\hat{q}^{e}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix},~~\hat{r}^{e}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(101)

Then combining the above results and putting them back to (
99
), we establish that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}={\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2}+\hat{r}^{e}-2\hat{q}^{e\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\hat{r}^{e})+2(h-\hat{q}^{e})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*})+(b-\beta^{*})^{\intercal}\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]+\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\right)\mathbf{G}(b-\beta^{*}),

where the second equality holds due to Lemma
1
.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
3

Since the spectral norm is sub-multiplicative, it follows from (
101
) that the following inequalities hold simultaneously:

\|\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\|_{2}\leq\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\|_{2},~~\|\hat{q}^{e}\|_{2}\leq\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{2}\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\|_{2},~~|\hat{r}^{e}|\leq\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\|_{2}

(102)

For the term
\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}
, notice that it is related to the covariance estimation, and a standard concentration inequality can be applied to upper bound
\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\|_{2}

[
Vershynin, 2018
, Theorem 4.7.1, Example 4.7.3]
.

It follows from Condition
2
that
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},(\varepsilon^{e}_{X})^{\intercal})^{\intercal}
is a sub-gaussian random vector. Then for any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
, we have

\left\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\right\|_{2}\leq CK^{2}\left({\sqrt{\frac{p+1+t}{n_{e}}}}+\frac{p+1+t}{n_{e}}\right)\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\right]\right\|_{2},

where
C,K
are some universal constants. Next, we utilize the union bound to control
\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\|_{2}
. We define
n=\min_{e\in\mathcal{E}}n_{e}
as the smallest sample size across all environments. For any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-t}
,

\displaystyle\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left({\sqrt{\frac{p+1+t}{{n}}}}+\frac{p+1+t}{{n}}\right)\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{e}_{Y}\\
\varepsilon^{e}_{X}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\right]\right\|_{2},

Since
(\varepsilon^{e}_{Y},(\varepsilon^{e}_{X})^{\intercal})^{\intercal}
is a sub-gaussian random vector with a bounded second moment for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we shall simplify the above inequality as follows: for any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-t}
,

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\hat{\mathbf{\Omega}}^{e}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\left({\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{{n}}}}+\frac{p+t}{{n}}\right).

Together with the result in the preceding result and (
102
), we establish that: with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-t}
:

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\max\{\|\hat{\mathbf{P}}^{e}\|_{2},\|\hat{q}^{e}\|_{2},|\hat{r}^{e}|\}\lesssim\left({\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{{n}}}}+\frac{p+t}{{n}}\right).

E.4
Proof of Lemma
4

For each environment
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we define the empirical risk as

g_{e}(b)=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{e}-b^{\intercal}X^{e}]^{2},

and we abbreviate
g(b):=(g_{e}(b))_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{E}|}
. We define the function as

f(b,w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)g_{e}(b)-\mu\|w\|_{2}^{2}.

Differentiating
f
w.r.t.
b
and
w
gives two gradient blocks:

\nabla_{b}f(b,w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b),\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{w}f(b,w)=g(b)-2\mu w.

Hence, the full gradient is

\nabla f(b,w)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{b}f(b,w)\\
\nabla_{w}f(b,w)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b)\\
g(b)-2\mu w\end{pmatrix}.

According to Condition
3
,
g_{e}(b)
is
M
-smooth with respect to
b
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
such that

\|\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{1})-\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{2})\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{1}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2},

and we work on a bounded parameter set
b\in\mathcal{B}
with bounded gradient norm

\kappa_{2}:=\sup_{e\in\mathcal{E},b\in\mathcal{B}}\|\nabla g_{e}(b)\|_{2}<\infty.

Our goal is to show that for any two points
(b_{1},w_{1}),(b_{2},w_{2})
,

\|\nabla f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla f(b_{2},w_{2})\|_{2}\leq L\sqrt{\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}},

for
L=2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu
.

Step-1: Variation of the
b
-gradient.

Using the triangle inequality, we shall split the difference as follows:

\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{2})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}+\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{2})\right\|_{2}.

Next, we upper bound the two terms on the right-hand side in order.

(a)

Change in
b
with fixed
w
.

For the first term,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{2})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(w_{2e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\left(\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{1})-\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{2})\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left|w_{2e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right|\left\|\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{1})-\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{2})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\kappa_{1}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2},

where the last inequality holds as
g_{e}(b)
is
\kappa_{1}
-smooth with

\|\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{1})-\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{2})\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{1}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2},

and

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left|w_{2e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right|\leq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}w_{2e}+\frac{\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\leq 2.

(b)

Change in
w
with fixed
b
.

For the second term,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{2})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}(w_{1e}-w_{2e})\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{1})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{2}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{1}\leq\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2},

where the first inequality holds due to
\kappa_{2}:=\sup_{e\in\mathcal{E},b\in\mathcal{B}}\|\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b)\|_{2}
.

Combining
(a)
and
(b)
, we establish that

\left\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}\leq 2\kappa_{1}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}+\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}.

(103)

Step-2: Variation of the
w
-gradient.

Similarly, we shall decompose:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{w}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{w}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|g(b_{1})-g(b_{2})-2\mu(w_{1}-w_{2})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|g(b_{1})-g(b_{2})\|_{2}+2\mu\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}.

For each coordinate
e
,

g_{e}(b_{1})-g_{e}(b_{2})=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{b}g_{e}(b_{2}+t(b_{1}-b_{2}))^{\intercal}(b_{1}-b_{2})dt.

Taking norms and using the bounded gradient assumption gives

\|g(b_{1})-g(b_{2})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\kappa_{2}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}.

Therefore,

\left\|\nabla_{w}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{w}f(b_{2},w_{2})\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\kappa_{2}\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}+2\mu\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}.

(104)

Step-3: Joint Smoothness.

Stacking both blocks from (
103
) and (
104
),

\displaystyle\|\nabla f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla f(b_{2},w_{2})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{b}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{b}f(b_{2},w_{2})\|_{2}+\|\nabla_{w}f(b_{1},w_{1})-\nabla_{w}f(b_{2},w_{2})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\kappa_{1}+\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\kappa_{2}\right)\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}+(\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu)\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\rm joint}\sqrt{\|b_{1}-b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}},

where

L_{\rm joint}\leq\sqrt{\left(2\kappa_{1}+\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\kappa_{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}\kappa_{2}+2\mu\right)^{2}}\leq 2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu.

Hence,
f(b,w)
is
L_{\rm joint}
-smooth with
L_{\rm joint}\leq 2\kappa_{1}+2\kappa_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{E}|}+2\mu
.

E.5
Proof of Lemma
5

We denote by
y^{*}(x)
the unique maximizer:

y^{*}(x)=\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(x,y).

The uniqueness of
y^{*}(x)
follows from the strong concavity of
f(x,\cdot)
in
y
, for each fixed
x
.

Step-1: Optimality conditions.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the first-order optimality condition for the concave maximization problem gives:

(y-y^{*}(x))^{\intercal}\nabla_{y}f(x,y^{*}(x))\leq 0,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

Applying this condition to
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
:

\left(y-y^{*}(x_{1})\right)^{\intercal}\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{1}))\leq 0,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall y\in\mathcal{Y},

(105)

\left(y-y^{*}(x_{2})\right)^{\intercal}\nabla_{y}f(x_{2},y^{*}(x_{2}))\leq 0,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

(106)

Substituting
y=y^{*}(x_{2})
in (
105
) and
y=y^{*}(x_{1})
in (
106
) and adding them yields:

\left(y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\right)^{\intercal}\left(\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{1}))-\nabla_{y}f(x_{2},y^{*}(x_{2}))\right)\leq 0.

(107)

Step-2: Using strong concavity in
y
.

Since the function
f(x_{1},\cdot)
is
\mu
-strongly concave, its gradient satisfies:

\left(\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{2}))-\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{1}))\right)^{\intercal}(y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1}))\leq-\mu\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\|_{2}^{2}.

Adding the inequality above and (
107
) eliminates the middle term
\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{1}))
and yields

\left(y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\right)^{\intercal}\left(\nabla_{y}f(x_{2},y^{*}(x_{2}))-\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{2}))\right)\geq\mu\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\|_{2}^{2}.

(108)

Step-3: Bounding using joint smoothness

Because
f
is
\ell
-smooth jointly in
(x,y)
, varying
x
while keeping
y
fixed gives

\left\|\nabla_{y}f(x_{2},y^{*}(x_{2}))-\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{2}))\right\|\leq\ell\|x_{2}-x_{1}\|_{2}.

Combining with (
108
) and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\mu\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\right\|_{2}\left\|\nabla_{y}f(x_{2},y^{*}(x_{2}))-\nabla_{y}f(x_{1},y^{*}(x_{2}))\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\ell\left\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\right\|_{2}\|x_{2}-x_{1}\|_{2}.

Diving both sides by
\mu\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\|_{2}
(nonzero since
\mu>0
) yields

\|y^{*}(x_{2})-y^{*}(x_{1})\|_{2}\leq\frac{\ell}{\mu}\|x_{2}-x_{1}\|_{2}.

(109)

Thus, the maximizer mapping
y^{*}(\cdot)
is
\ell/\mu
-Lipschitz continuous in
x
.

Step-4: Smoothness of the value function.

We define

F(x)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(x,y).

By Danskin’s theorem (which applies since
\mathcal{Y}
is convex and bounded, and
y^{*}(x)
is uniquely defined),

\nabla F(x)=\nabla_{x}f(x,y^{*}(x)).

Then for any
x_{1},x_{2}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(x^{\prime})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\nabla_{x}f(x,y^{*}(x))-\nabla_{x}f(x^{\prime},y^{*}(x^{\prime}))\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ell\left(\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|y^{*}(x)-y^{*}(x^{\prime})\|_{2}\right).

where the inequality follows from the
\ell
-smoothness of
f
jointly on
(x,y)
.

Leveraging the Lipschitz property of
y^{*}(\cdot)
in (
109
) gives

\left\|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(x^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq(\ell+\ell^{2}/\mu)\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Hence
F(x)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(x,y)
is
(\ell+\ell^{2}/\mu)
-smooth in
x
.

E.6
Proof of Lemma
6

The first part is one of the multiple versions of the celebrated Danskin’s theorem
[
Danskin, 1966
]
, and it was proven in
Bertsekas [1997, Proposition B.25]
. Here, we only show the second part of removing the
{\rm conv}
operation.

To remove the
{\rm conv}
operation, it suffices to show that

A(x)=\left\{\frac{\partial f(x,z)}{\partial x}:~~z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)\right\}

itself has already been a convex set. For any
a_{1},a_{2}\in A(x)
, it holds that

a_{1}=\frac{\partial f(x,\bar{z}_{1})}{\partial x},~~a_{2}=\frac{\partial f(x,\bar{z}_{2})}{\partial x}

for
\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x).
Since
f(x,z)
is linear function towards
z
, it holds that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(x,z)
is a linear function with respect to
z
as well. Then for any
t\in[0,1]
, it holds that

ta_{1}+(1-t)a_{2}=t\frac{\partial f(x,\bar{z}_{1})}{\partial x}+(1-t)\frac{\partial f(x,\bar{z}_{2})}{\partial x}=\frac{\partial f(x,t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2})}{\partial x}.

If we have
t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}(x)
, the preceding equation implies that
ta_{1}+(1-t)a_{2}\in A(x)
for any
t\in[0,1]
, meaning that
A(x)
is a convex set.
Therefore, to show that
A(x)
is a convex set, it suffices to show that
t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

By the definition of
\bar{z}_{1}
and
\bar{z}_{2}
, we know that

f(x,\bar{z}_{1})=f(x,\bar{z}_{2})=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x,z).

As
\mathcal{Z}
is a convex set,
t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}
as well. Moreover, since
f(x,z)
is a linear function with respect to
z
for every
x
, we have

tf(x,\bar{z}_{1})+(1-t)f(x,\bar{z}_{2})=f(x,t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2})=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x,z),

for every
t\in[0,1].
It implies that
t\bar{z}_{1}+(1-t)\bar{z}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as well. The proof is completed.

E.7
Proof of Lemma
7

By the definition of
\hat{\phi}(b,w)
in (
69
), its Hessian matrix towards
b
is:

\nabla_{b}^{2}\hat{\phi}(b,w)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left({w}_{e}-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma|\mathcal{E}|}\right)\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}.

Since
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}
, it holds that:

\nabla_{b}^{2}\hat{\phi}(b,w)\succeq-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}.

(110)

We observe that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]+\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}.

Therefore, we establish that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}\right\|_{2}

(111)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{E}|\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{E}|\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}+|\mathcal{E}|\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}.

By the standard concentration inequality for covariance matrix estimation
Vershynin [2018, Theorem 4.7.1, Example 4.7.3]
, for any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t}
, we have

\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}\leq CK^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{n_{e}}}+\frac{p+t}{n_{e}}\right)\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2},

(112)

where
C,K
are some universal constants.
We define
{n}=\min_{e\in\mathcal{E}}n_{e}
the smallest sample size across environments.
By union bound, it follows from (
112
) that for any
t\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-t}
:

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}\leq CK^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{{n}}}+\frac{p+t}{{n}}\right)\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}.

(113)

Putting the results in (
111
) and (
113
) together, we obtain that

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\left\|\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{n_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}x^{e}_{i}(x^{e}_{i})^{\intercal}\right\|_{2}\leq\left[1+CK^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{{n}}}+\frac{p+t}{{n}}\right)\right]\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}.

Together with the above inequality with (
110
), we shall establish that: for any
t>0
, with probability at least
1-2|\mathcal{E}|e^{-t}

\nabla_{b}^{2}\hat{\phi}(b,w)\succeq-\left[1+CK^{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{{n}}}+\frac{p+t}{{n}}\right)\right]\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}.

According to Lemma
1
,
\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]
shall be expressed as follows:

\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]=\mathbf{G}^{\intercal}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}\mathbb{E}[\eta\eta^{\intercal}]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{YX}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}+\mathbb{E}[{\delta^{e}}{\delta^{e\intercal}}]\right)\mathbf{G}.

Together with the bounded second moments of the vector
\eta
and
{\delta^{e}}
, for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, as implied in Condition
2
, we obtain that

\max_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left\|\mathbb{E}[X^{e}(X^{e})^{\intercal}]\right\|_{2}<C
bounded as well. We consider
{n}\geq p+t
, then

\sqrt{\frac{p+t}{n}}+\frac{p+t}{n}\leq 2\frac{p+t}{n}\leq 2.

The preceding results imply that

\nabla_{b}^{2}\hat{\phi}(b,w)\succeq-C,

for some universal constant
C>0.

Therefore, for any weak convexity parameter
\rho>C
, we know that the function

b\mapsto\hat{\phi}(b,w)+\frac{1}{2}\|b\|^{2}

is a convex function towards
b
, and
\hat{\phi}(b,w)
is
\rho
-weakly convex towards
b
for any
w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}.

Recall the definition of
\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\max_{w\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{E}|}}\hat{\phi}(b,w)
, it can be written as:
